L1: 4. Proportion of subjects with Valsalva LVOT-G &lt;30 mm Hg at Week 24.5. Duration of septal reduction therapy (SRT) eligibility up to 24 weeks of treatment for those who were SRT at baseline.6. KCCQ-CSS change from baseline at Week 12.7. Proportion of subjects with &gt;1 NYHA functional class improvement at Week 12.8. Valsalva LVOT-G change from baseline at Week 12.9. Proportion of subjects with Valsalva LVOT-G &lt;30 mm Hg at Week 12.10. Change from baseline in total workload during cardiopulmonary exercise testing (CPET).
L2: Baseline characteristics were similar between the treatment groups.
L3: The pVO2 primary endpoint result showed a least-squares (LS) mean difference (95% confidence interval) of 1.7 (1.0, 2.4) for aficamten compared to placebo, p-value &lt; 0.0001. The treatment effect on the primary endpoint was generally consistent across various predefined subgroups based on demographic characteristics, baseline disease characteristics, and concomitant medications.
L4: The results of all the secondary efficacy endpoints listed above were also statistically significant.
L5: The clinical utility of pVO2 is the ability to assess functional capacity and the effectiveness of an intervention in cardiomyopathy trials, and thus, it is an accepted endpoint for a regulatory decision. The observed treatment effect on pVO2 was interpreted to be clinically meaningful (see Section 6.3.1). This finding was accompanied by a large treatment effect of aficamten on LVOT-G as well as clinically meaningful improvement on functional capacity and symptoms assessed with the KCCQ-CSS and NYHA functional class.
L6: In summary, the SEQUOIA-HCM trial provided evidence from an adequate and well controlled clinical investigation that aficamten improves functional capacity and symptoms in subjects with symptomatic oHCM as measured by pVO2, KCCQ-CSS and NYHA class.
L7: REDWOOD-HCM Study
L8: REDWOOD-HCM was a Phase 2, multi-center, randomized, placebo-controlled, dose-finding study that evaluated the safety, tolerability, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of aficamten in adults with symptomatic oHCM. The study was performed at 23 centers in Italy, Netherlands, Spain, and the United States. Subjects with oHCM and LVOT-G ≥30 mm Hg at rest or ≥50 mm Hg with Valsalva were randomized 2:1 to receive aficamten (n=28) or placebo (n=13) in 2 dose-finding cohorts (i.e., 14 subjects per dose-finding cohort). Doses were titrated, up to 3 escalating doses, based on LVOT-G and left ventricular ejection fraction (LVEF).
L9: Efficacy was evaluated by echocardiography (i.e., LVOT-G, LVEF), NYHA class, and patient-reported outcome quality of life assessment. Results showed a clinically meaningful reduction in LVOT-G in the combined aficamten cohorts compared to placebo, at rest (-25 mm Hg) and post-Valsalva (-41 mm Hg) at Week 10, providing confirmatory evidence of effectiveness of aficamten to reduce LVOT-G in symptomatic oHCM subjects.
L10: 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L11: 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L12: Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework
L13: DimensionEvidence and UncertaintiesConclusions and Reasons



Analysis of condition

• Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM) is a rare genetic myocardial disorder characterized by left ventricular (LV) hypertrophy, hyperdynamic contraction, and impaired relaxation.
• The prevalence of asymptomatic HCM among young adults in the United States has been estimated at 1 in 200 to 1 in 500, with symptomatic HCM being less common based on medical claims data (estimated at lower than 1 in 3,000).
• About 70% of individuals diagnosed with HCM have obstructive HCM (oHCM) with a prevalence of 2-5 per 100,000 people. The presence of left ventricular outflow tract (LVOT) obstruction is an important prognostic factor in HCM.
• Complications of oHCM include arrhythmias, dilated cardiomyopathy, mitral regurgitation, heart failure (HF), stroke, and sudden death from ventricular tachycardia/ventricular fibrillation. The incidence of sudden death is approximately 1% per year, often in people &lt;30 years of age. The cause of death from oHCM is age-dependent (sudden death in the younger population, HF in middle age, and stroke in the elderly).
• Patients with oHCM have a wide range of symptoms and disease courses. The typical symptoms of oHCM include dyspnea, reduced exercise tolerance, angina, palpitations, and syncope. Some patients suffer from high symptom burden causing significant debilitating consequences, thus profoundly affecting their quality of life.


oHCM is a rare, chronic, progressive disease characterized by cardiomyocyte disarray, hypercontractile myocardium, and impaired LV relaxation with variable clinical presentations and clinical course. oHCM is associated with significant cardiovascular-related morbidity and mortality, including functional limitation and risk of sudden cardiac death.
L14: DimensionEvidence and UncertaintiesConclusions and Reasons



Current treatment options

• First-line, guideline-directed pharmacologic management of symptomatic patients with oHCM includes beta-blockers and non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers (off-label use)
              
– Propranolol is the only beta-blocker approved for improving NYHA functional class in patients with oHCM.


• Patients with oHCM who do not respond to first-line therapy are candidates for advanced pharmacologic therapy including disopyramide and cardiac myosin inhibitors
              
– The first-in-class cardiac myosin inhibitor, mavacamten, was approved by the FDA in 2022 for treatment of adults with symptomatic oHCM to improve functional capacity and symptoms.
– Mavacamten has substantial drug-drug interactions (DDIs) and prolonged half-life that increase the risk of systolic dysfunction and HF. It is only available through a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy (REMS) program.


• Septal reduction therapy (SRT) with either surgical myectomy or alcohol septal ablation is generally reserved for drug-refractory symptoms or intolerant adverse effects from pharmacologic therapy
              
– Although these procedures are generally effective, they have important risks and access to the procedures may be limited.




There remains an unmet need for additional effective and safe treatments for patients with symptomatic oHCM to address the impact of the disease.


Benefit

• The effectiveness of aficamten for the treatment of adults with oHCM was evaluated in the SEQUOIA-HCM trial, a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicenter, international, parallel-group study in patients with symptomatic oHCM.
• The primary efficacy endpoint was change from baseline in pVO2 at Week 24. At baseline, mean pVO2 was 18.5 mL/kg/min. The study met its primary endpoint showing greater increase in pVO2 with aficamten compared to placebo with a LS mean difference (95% confidence interval) of 1.7 (1.0, 2.4), p-value&lt;0.0001.
• The clinical meaningfulness of the observed treatment effect on pVO2 of 1.7 mL/min/kg between groups was supported by the following exploratory analyses:
              
– The empirical cumulative density function curves of changes in pVO2 by NYHA class groups




SEQUOIA-HCM demonstrated statistically persuasive and clinically meaningful treatment effect of aficamten versus placebo on pVO2, KCCQ-CSS, NYHA functional class, LVOT-G, and days eligible for SRT. These data support effectiveness of aficamten to improve functional capacity and symptoms in patients with symptomatic oHCM.
L15: DimensionEvidence and UncertaintiesConclusions and Reasons





- A greater proportion of subjects achieving a composite endpoint of pVO2 and NYHA class improvement in the aficamten versus placebo group (42% versus 14%)
- Statistically significant and clinically meaningful treatment effects on all secondary endpoints, including those that assessed symptoms and functional capacity (KCCQ-CSS, NYHA functional class), LVOT-G and days eligible for SRT.
• Subgroup analyses of the primary efficacy endpoint by baseline characteristics including beta-blocker use at baseline and stratification factors showed a consistent effect.



Risk and risk management

• Safety evaluation of aficamten was primarily based on the data obtained in the SEQUOIA-HCM trial (N=282 receiving at least one dose of study drug; n=142 in the aficamten group and n=140 in the placebo group). This study provided controlled data with a median exposure of 6 months. Long-term safety is supported by the open-label extension Study CY 6022. With the updated safety data through August 31, 2024, the median exposure for all aficamten-treated patients was about 11 months.
• Safety evaluation focused on the on-target effects of aficamten, whereby reduction of contractility could increase the risk of HF with reduced ejection fraction.
              
- There was a small decrease in LVEF (~4 to 5% with an SD of 7%) during 24 weeks of aficamten treatment compared with placebo. LVEF decline appeared reversible with drug discontinuation.
- The incidence of LVEF &lt;50% was low (4 to 5% assessed by core laboratory or site-read), and none of the subjects who experienced LVEF &lt;50% had associated signs and symptoms of HF except one with reported adverse event (AE) of HF 4 weeks prior to the reduction of LVEF.
- No aficamten-treated oHCM patients experienced a serious HF event or severe systolic dysfunction (LVEF &lt;30%) in SEQUOIA-HCM and the extension study.
- Hypertension was the only AE that occurred at a frequency of ≥5% higher in the aficamten group compared to the placebo group (8% versus 2%).
- There was a greater mean change from baseline in both systolic and diastolic blood pressure in the aficamten group versus placebo group at




The principal safety concern of aficamten is the risk of HF due to systolic dysfunction resulting from an excessive pharmacologic effect of aficamten. In SEQUOIA-HCM, in which regular pharmacodynamic (PD) monitoring via echocardiogram was implemented, the risk of HF and/or systolic dysfunction was low during 28 weeks of treatment. The risk remained low in the ongoing extension study in patients with oHCM.
We determined that an elements to assure safe use (ETASU) REMS is required to assure safe use of aficamten for the following reasons: (1) an iatrogenic risk of HF with aficamten exists based on the mechanism of action of the drug; (2) the total exposure to aficamten to date remains relatively low (~300 oHCM patients); and (3) the risk is likely to be higher in the postmarket compared to trial setting (in the postmarket setting aficamten is likely to be used in a wider range of patients with higher comorbidities and where clinical monitoring is less rigorous).
L16: DimensionEvidence and UncertaintiesConclusions and Reasons




the end of the 24-week treatment period (2/3 mm Hg versus -3/-1 mm Hg, respectively).

Concomitant use of potent inhibitors of multiple CYP enzymes, including CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP3A4, such as fluconazole and fluvoxamine, with aficamten may increase aficamten exposure and increase the risk of HF and/or systolic dysfunction. Dose modification is required when concurrent use of these drugs occurs.
There are insufficient human pregnancy data in the aficamten-exposed subjects to inform the safety of aficamten use in pregnancy. The animal reproductive toxicity data do not suggest a serious risk of developmental toxicity during pregnancy.


The only observed adverse reaction with aficamten was hypertension. Aficamten-associated increases in blood pressure are consistent with the hemodynamic consequence of attenuating the LVOT obstruction. This risk can be managed through labeling.
To further assess safety of aficamten during pregnancy, we will issue a postmarketing requirement for a descriptive pregnancy safety study and lactation study to collect prospective and retrospective data in women exposed to aficamten during pregnancy and/or lactation.
L17: Abbreviations: CYP, cytochrome P450; KCCQ-CSS, Kansas City Cardiomyopathy Questionnaire-Clinical Summary Score; N, number of subjects; n, number of subjects in specific group; LVEF, left ventricular ejection fraction; NYHA, New York Heart Association; pVO2, peak oxygen consumption
L18: 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L19: In the phase 3 trial, aficamten improved peak oxygen consumption (an assessment of functional capacity) over 24 weeks compared to placebo in adults with oHCM. While the mean treatment effect size appears modest (i.e., treatment difference of 1.7 mL/kg/min at Week 24 from a baseline mean of 18.5 mL/kg/min), these changes are accompanied by a large reduction in LVOT-G (mean treatment effect of -48 mmHg on Valsalva LVOT-G at Week 24 from a baseline mean of 83 mmHg) as well as benefits on patient symptoms and functional capacity assessed using KCCQ-CSS (mean treatment effect of 7 from a baseline mean of ~75) and NYHA functional class (59% improvement with aficamten at Week 24 compared to 24% improvement with placebo). We conclude that aficamten clearly improves symptoms and functional capacity in adults with oHCM.
L20: Due to aficamten's mechanism of action the main concern is a risk of precipitating HF, although the risk appears low when echocardiography is used to monitor LVEF and guide dose titration. There is also drug-drug interaction potential, which can be managed by adjusting the aficamten dose and/or avoiding coadministration.
L21: Aficamten's benefits on patient symptoms and functional capacity outweigh the risk of HF when the risk is mitigated with an ETASU REMS, which will help ensure patients on aficamten have regular echocardiograms to monitor for reduced LVEF and guide dose titration. The drug-drug interaction potential can be adequately mitigated with labeling, that describes the scenarios for dose adjustment and/or avoidance of aficamten. There is a risk of hypertension, consistent with the reduced LVOT, that can also be adequately managed with labeling, informing prescribers of this risk, which can be assessed with periodic blood pressure monitoring (which is routine in clinical care) and treated, as needed. Patients will also be informed of these risks through a Medication Guide, which is required to be dispensed with each new prescription and refill.
L22: Based on these considerations, we conclude that the overall benefit-risk assessment is favorable for aficamten for the treatment of adults with symptomatic oHCM to improve functional capacity and symptoms and that aficamten should be approved.
L23: II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L24: 3. Introduction
L25: NDA 219083 relies on a single, Phase 3 trial, SEQUOIA-HCM (CY 6031) and a Phase 2, dose-finding study, REDWOOD-HCM to support the following proposed indication for aficamten:
L26: “AFICAMTEN is a cardiac myosin inhibitor indicated for the treatment of adults with symptomatic obstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (oHCM)”
L27: The Applicant also has an ongoing long-term, single-arm, open-label extension study to assess the safety and durability of aficamten in patients with hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM).
L28: Disease Background (Autore et al. 2005; Maron et al. 2006; Ommen et al. 2024)
L29: Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy is an autosomal dominant genetic heart disease that affects men and women globally and is characterized by left ventricular (LV) hypertrophy, hyperdynamic contraction and impaired relaxation. The prevalence of asymptomatic HCM among young adults in the United States has been estimated to be approximately 1 in 500, with symptomatic HCM based on medical claims data being less common (estimated at &lt;1 in 3000) (1). A large percentage of patients are asymptomatic and/or undiagnosed.
L30: There are two HCM phenotypes based on the presence or absence of obstruction of the LVOT, oHCM and non-obstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (nHCM), where obstruction is defined as a peak LVOT gradient \geq 30 mm Hg at rest or \geq 50 mm Hg with provocation (e.g., Valsalva maneuver). Approximately 70% of individuals diagnosed with HCM have oHCM (2). The health impacts of HCM are variable with a wide range of symptoms and disease courses.
L31: Many patients experience minimal or few symptoms and minor or no limitations in daily activities; however, others suffer significant debilitating consequences and high symptom burden, most frequently dyspnea, chest pain and palpitations with profound effects on their lives. The presence of LVOT obstruction is an important prognostic factor in HCM (3). HCM-related adverse outcomes include sudden death, progressively limiting symptoms caused by diastolic dysfunction and/or LVOT obstruction, heart failure (HF) caused by systolic dysfunction, and thromboembolic complications.
L32: First-line, guideline-directed pharmacologic management of oHCM includes beta-blockers and non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers. Patients with oHCM who do not respond to first-line therapy are candidates for advanced pharmacologic therapy including disopyramide and cardiac myosin inhibitors. The first-in-class and only approved cardiac myosin inhibitor is mavacamten, approved by the FDA in 2022 for treatment of adults with symptomatic oHCM to improve functional capacity and symptoms. However, mavacamten has substantial drug-drug interactions (DDIs) and a long half-life that increase the risk of systolic dysfunction and heart failure. Mavacamten is available only through a REMS program. Septal reduction therapy with either surgical myectomy or alcohol septal ablation is generally reserved for drug-refractory symptoms or intolerant adverse effects from pharmacologic therapy. Although SRT is effective, it is only available in experienced HCM centers and there are potential procedural complications, such as spill-over (alcohol infusion into other arteries causing an expanded myocardial
L33: infarction), disruption of electrical conduction causing blocks and potential pacemaker requirements, and resurgence of LVOT obstruction requiring a repeat procedure. There continues to be an unmet need for a disease-specific drug to treat symptomatic oHCM.
L34: Drug Class
L35: Aficamten (also referred to as CK-3773274), is a small molecule, allosteric and reversible inhibitor of cardiac myosin. Aficamten reduces the force generated by myosin at the cardiac sarcomere. In oHCM patients, myosin inhibition with aficamten reduces cardiac sarcomere contractility and LVOT obstruction.

L74: 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L75: 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L76: 5.1.1. Mechanism of Action
L77: Aficamten is a small molecule inhibitor of cardiac myosin intended for the treatment of adults with symptomatic oHCM. oHCM is characterized by left ventricular hypertrophy and hypercontractility, with impaired relaxation and filling during diastole. This causes abnormal hemodynamics and clinical symptoms such as chest pain, fatigue, and syncope. By specifically inhibiting cardiac myosin, a sarcomeric molecular motor in cardiomyocytes, aficamten reduces the excessive contractility that contributes to the pathophysiology of oHCM.
L78: At a molecular level, cardiomyocyte contractility is driven by the cyclic interaction between myosin and actin at the cardiac sarcomere. Myosin and actin interactions are regulated by calcium, the presence of which allows myosin's motor head domain to bind actin. Subsequent adenosine triphosphate (ATP) hydrolysis powers sarcomere shortening and cardiomyocyte contraction (Figure 1). The myosin-actin complex dissociates after each power stroke, which primes the system for another round of contraction. Aficamten binds directly to the head domain of cardiac myosin and is more selective for the cardiac \beta-myosin heavy chain isoform (Moussavi-Harami and Regnier 2024), which is predominantly expressed in the ventricles. Aficamten binding stabilizes myosin in an off-actin conformation, decreasing the number of functional myosin heads entering a force-generating state, resulting in reduced cardiomyocyte contractility (Figure 1).
L79: Figure 1. Schematic Illustration of the Mechanism of Action of Aficamten in Obstructive Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy
L80: Figure 1: Schematic Illustration of the Mechanism of Action of Aficamten in Obstructive Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy. The figure is divided into three panels: A, B, and C. Panel A, titled 'Cardiac Hypercontractility', shows a schematic of actin-myosin cross-bridges with an upward arrow indicating an increased number of cross-bridges. Panel B, titled 'LVOT Obstruction in HCM', shows a heart diagram and an echocardiogram with text indicating LVOT obstruction ≥ 30 mm Hg MR, increased wall stress, and diastolic dysfunction. It also shows increased heart failure (HF) symptoms and decreased quality of life (QoL). Panel C, titled 'Aficamten binds reversibly to cardiac myosin', shows a schematic of actin-myosin cross-bridges with a downward arrow indicating a decreased number of cross-bridges. Arrows indicate a flow from Panel A to Panel B, and from Panel B to Panel C.
L81: Source: Adopted from (Maron et al. 2023)
L82: Abbreviations: HCM, hypertrophic cardiomyopathy; HF, heart failure; LVOT, left ventricular outflow tract; QoL, quality of life
L83: 5.1.2. In Vitro and In Vivo Pharmacology
L84: Experiments in vitro with myofibrils from various muscle types across species and cultured cardiomyocytes interrogated the primary pharmacology and selectivity of aficamten. Detailed summaries of individual studies are provided in Section 13.1 of this review. Aficamten decreased the ATPase activity of bovine cardiac and slow muscle myofibrils with similar half maximal inhibitory concentration (IC_{50}) values, due to the same myosin isoform (MYH7) being expressed in each muscle type but was a weak inhibitor of both rabbit fast muscle myofibrils and chicken gizzard smooth muscle (see Table 48). These results show that aficamten spares smooth muscle and fast-twitch skeletal muscle contractility at concentrations that inhibit cardiac and slow-twitch skeletal muscle contractility in vitro. Notably, no signals related to skeletal muscle weakness were observed in in vivo nonclinical studies or in clinical trials. Cardiac myosin proteins are highly evolutionarily conserved, allowing comparison of data across myofibril preparations from different species, which allows for clinically relevant nonclinical pharmacology studies in animals. Aficamten inhibits contractility without impacting intracellular cardiomyocyte calcium concentrations, demonstrated in vitro in both rat ventricular cardiomyocytes and human cardiomyocytes derived from induced pluripotent stem cells, suggesting low risk for arrhythmias with clinical use. Aficamten potency was similar in engineered heart tissues derived from induced pluripotent stem cells from a normal donor (IC_{50}=1.08\mu\text{M}) and those from a donor heterozygous for MYH7 R403Q (IC_{50}=1.65\mu\text{M}), demonstrating its efficacy against a cardiac myosin mutation associated with hypertrophic cardiomyopathy and its potential to treat HCM of multiple etiologies.
L85: Prospect of clinical benefit was shown using heterozygous transgenic MYH7 R403Q mice with HCM, and a cat model of hereditary oHCM (A31P MYBPC3 mutation), using aficamten doses comparable to those used in toxicology studies. Notably, the transgenic mouse develops LV thickening without LVOT obstruction, while the cat model reliably reflects human oHCM symptoms including LVOT obstruction. Aficamten dose-dependently reduced left ventricular fractional shortening (LVFS) compared to baseline after a single dose in both disease models. In vivo pharmacology studies were also conducted in healthy rats and dogs without HCM. In healthy rats, IC_{10} (the plasma concentration to result in a 10% reduction in LVFS) was observed at 270 ng/mL and IC_{50} at 2,662 ng/mL. In healthy dogs, the IC_{10} was observed at 60 ng/mL and IC_{50} at 430 ng/mL, with a slightly steeper dose response than rats.
L86: There was no major additional magnitude of the pharmacodynamic (PD) effect in healthy rats between a single dose or repeated dosing with aficamten, and the effects reversed 24 hours after the final dose. Reduced LVFS caused by aficamten was rapidly rescued in rats by administration of dobutamine, a \beta-adrenergic agonist, demonstrating a pharmacological method to reverse its action. The pharmacodynamics of aficamten was not significantly impacted in a heterozygous MYH7 R403Q mouse model of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, as the dose response relationship was similar to that observed in wild-type mice. Aficamten decreased the LVOT pressure gradient in a cat model of oHCM after a single dose, indicating improvement of mitral valve obstruction coinciding with reduced LVFS.
L87: Taken together, these data provided a therapeutic rationale for relieving LVOT obstruction in oHCM subjects treated with aficamten. Aficamten was shown to reversibly (non-covalently) inhibit the contractility of purified myofibrils, intact cultured cardiomyocytes, and hearts in vivo. Aficamten acts at the sarcomere, independently of electrophysiology. Aficamten was similarly
L88: NDA 219083MYQORZO (aficamten)
L89: potent between cardiomyocytes with wild type myosin and R403Q mutants, one of the most common clinical mutations associated with HCM. Aficamten had no appreciable activity against smooth muscle or skeletal fast-twitch muscle at clinically relevant concentrations but may theoretically have unintended effects on skeletal slow-twitch muscle myofibrils, although no safety signal has been identified. Aficamten reduced LVFS and LVOT pressure gradient in a cat model of oHCM, providing rationale for its development as a therapeutic.
L90: Overall, the nonclinical data submitted by the Applicant support the proposed therapeutic rationale for the development of aficamten for oHCM and provide adequate information to label its mechanism of action (Section 12.1 of the product prescribing information [PI]).
L91: 5.1.3. Established Pharmacologic Class and Rationale
L92: For Established Pharmacologic Class of aficamten, the Applicant proposed “cardiac myosin inhibitor,” which was used for Camzyos (mavacamten).
L93: Several lines of in vitro and in vivo evidence suggest that aficamten binds to the cardiac myosin motor and decreases its ATPase activity, providing a molecular basis of reducing cardiac contractility to treat hypertrophic cardiomyopathy. Thus, the use of “cardiac myosin inhibitor” as the Established Pharmacologic Class is supported. Aficamten would be the second-in-class cardiac myosin inhibitor, should it be approved. Mavacamten, the first-in-class, was approved for hypertrophic cardiomyopathy in 2023.

Established pharmacologic class (EPC)Cardiac myosin inhibitor

Mechanism of actionAficamten is an allosteric and reversible inhibitor of cardiac myosin motor activity. Aficamten reduces the force generated by myosin at the cardiac sarcomere, which contributes to the pathophysiology of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM). In HCM subjects, myosin inhibition with aficamten may reduce cardiac sarcomere contractility and left ventricular outflow tract (LVOT) obstruction.

Active moietiesAficamten

QT prolongationA lack of QTc prolongation across the therapeutic concentration range of aficamten was demonstrated (Study CY 6019). At a single dose of 50 mg (i.e., similar exposure as 20 mg once daily [QD] dosing to steady-state), the upper limit of the predicted placebo-corrected change from baseline in QT interval corrected by Fridericia (\Delta\Delta QTcF) 90% confidence interval (CI) for aficamten was &lt;10 msec.

L107: CharacteristicDrug Information



Elimination

Mass balance resultsWhen 20 mg (100 µCi) of 14C-aficamten was administered to healthy males, approximately 89.7% of the radioactive oral dose was recovered, with 32.0% excreted in urine and 57.7% excreted in feces. Aficamten was eliminated primarily by metabolism and, to a lesser extent, by fecal excretion, with renal excretion playing a minor role (Study CY 6013).

ClearanceThe population PK-derived clearance (CL/F) estimate of aficamten in subjects with oHCM was 2.62 L/hr (Population PK report CYT AFI-PMX-001).

Half-lifeAficamten has a terminal elimination half-life of approximately 80 hours (Population PK report CYT-AFI-PMX-001).

Metabolic pathway(s)Aficamten is primarily eliminated via CYP2C9-mediated metabolism (50%) with contributions from CYP3A (26%), CYP2D6, and CYP2C19 (Studies Nonclin-0029 and Nonclin-0451).

Primary excretion pathways (% dose)See Mass balance study results above.

Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations

Body weightBody weight is predicted to have a modest effect on aficamten pharmacokinetics. Aficamten plasma AUCtau, Cmax, and Ctau in a subject with oHCM at the fourth (i.e., highest) weight quartile were 34%, 35%, and 33% lower, respectively, relative to a subject with oHCM at the first (i.e., lowest) weight quartile (Population PK report CYT-AFI-PMX-001). No dose adjustment of aficamten is warranted based on body weight.

AgeAge was not identified as a statistically significant covariate on aficamten pharmacokinetics (Population PK report CYT-AFI-PMX-001).

Renal impairmentNo impact of mild or moderate renal impairment on aficamten pharmacokinetics was identified in population PK analysis based on the Phase 2 (Study CY 6021) and Phase 3 (Trial CY 6031) data (Population PK report CYT-AFI-PMX-001).

Hepatic impairmentThe exposure (i.e., Cmax and AUC) of aficamten were similar (i.e., no meaningful difference) in subjects with normal liver function and subjects with moderate hepatic impairment (i.e., Child-Pugh Class B) (Study CY 6017). No studies were conducted in subjects with severe hepatic impairment (i.e., Child-Pugh Class C).

Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Precipitant)

Inhibition/induction of metabolismIn vitro studies suggest aficamten does not substantially inhibit any of the CYP isoforms (Studies Nonclin-0033 and Nonclin-0052). Aficamten is not predicted to be an in vivo inducer in humans (Studies Nonclin-0016 and Nonclin-0494).

Inhibition/induction of transporter systemsAficamten is a weak P-gp inhibitor (Study CY 6014)

L110: 6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
L111: 6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential Effectiveness
L112: Applicant's Dose Selection for Phase 3 Trial
L113: The clinical development program sought to maintain a plasma concentration of aficamten required for desired efficacy, but avoiding supratherapeutic concentrations that may result in systolic dysfunction, leading to decrease in stroke volume and cardiac output, compensatory increases in heart rate, and potentially clinical heart failure. Therefore, the goal of dose selection was to find the lowest possible dose to safely achieve resting and Valsalva LVOT-G targets (i.e., resting LVOT-G of &lt;30 mm Hg and Valsalva LVOT-G of &lt;50 mm Hg) while maintaining normal LVEF (i.e., LVEF \geq 50\%).
L114: Dose selection for the Phase 3 development program was determined from a Phase 2, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, dose-finding study in subjects with oHCM (Study CY 6021, REDWOOD-HCM). The REDWOOD-HCM study had two dosing regimen cohorts and one placebo cohort. Subjects with symptomatic oHCM were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to aficamten or placebo and received up to 3 escalating doses of aficamten (i.e., 5, 10, and 15 mg in Cohort 1 and 10, 20, and 30 mg in Cohort 2) or placebo once daily for 10 weeks. Dose escalation in the aficamten cohorts was based on echocardiographic guidance. Subjects maintained background therapy including beta-blockers and/or calcium channel blockers, either as monotherapy or combined, but excluding the use of disopyramide given its unique safety profile and the potential impacts on the studied outcomes.
L115: Study CY 6021 was conducted with an objective to treat with the lowest possible dose to safely achieve resting and Valsalva LVOT-G targets and maintain normal LVEF. The 5 mg starting dose was demonstrated to be pharmacodynamically effective and was well-tolerated by all subjects. There were 11 out of 41 subjects from Cohorts 1 and 2 who required doses higher than 10 mg to achieve target LVOT-G. Two subjects were escalated up to 30 mg (Table 8) but one of the two subjects on the 30-mg dose was down-titrated to 20 mg due to LVEF &lt; 50\%, and the other subjects did not achieve an adequate PD response. Based on these data the Applicant concluded that the 30-mg dose is not likely to add substantial clinical benefit versus risk; therefore, 20 mg was selected as the highest dose in the Phase 3 trial. The Applicant's dose selection for the Phase 3 trial was deemed reasonable.

L121: Evaluation of the Phase 3 Trial Dosage Regimen
L122: The Phase 3 trial (CY 6031, SEQUOIA-HCM) dose regimen was evaluated using two complementary methods. First, exposure-response (E-R) analyses were performed to identify and quantify the relationship between steady-state aficamten exposure derived from population pharmacokinetic (PK) modeling and key primary, secondary, and exploratory efficacy and safety endpoints (see Section 14.5.2). Second, PK/PD modeling of the relationship between aficamten pharmacokinetics and the primary echocardiographic measurements for dose titration, Valsalva LVOT-G, and LVEF, was performed (see Section 14.5.1).
L123: The Applicant's population PK (PPK) model described the aficamten serum concentration-time profiles adequately following the administration of aficamten in subjects with oHCM. The PPK model was assessed as acceptable for simulating post hoc exposure metrics, e.g., area under the concentration-time curve (AUC)tau, maximum plasma concentration (Cmax), and trough plasma concentration (Ctau) of aficamten for PK/PD and E-R analyses of efficacy and safety measurements. The PK/PD model of aficamten was adequate to link aficamten exposure to LVEF and LVOT-G for aficamten-treated subjects from the REDWOOD-HCM study and Trial CY 6031. The results were used to support the proposed commercial dose regimen. The pharmacometrics reviewer conducted independent analyses to evaluate the frequency of echocardiographic assessment during titration and maintenance phases. The final PK/PD analysis is comprised of a curve with LVEF or LVOT-G declining in a slightly exponential manner with increasing aficamten exposure and is supportive of the proposed dose titration scheme. Refer to the following section below. Except for the probability of ≥1 NYHA class improvement at Week 24 and change in N-terminal pro-B-type natriuretic peptide (NT-proBNP) at Week 24, the results of exposure-efficacy analyses did not show a significant E-R relationship in the aficamten-treated group. The results of exposure-safety analyses did not show a trend between aficamten exposure (Cavg,ss and Cmax,ss) and safety endpoints such as major adverse cardiovascular events, cardiovascular adverse events, or hypertension (please refer to Section 14.5).
L124: In the Phase 3 trial, CY 6031, the efficacy and safety for aficamten 5 mg - 20 mg has been adequately demonstrated. In conclusion, the Applicant's selection of the Phase 3 dosage regimen was appropriate.
L125: Dose Titration Scheme
L126: Phase 2 and 3 Development
L127: In both Phase 2 and 3 studies, an individualized echocardiography-guided dose titration strategy was consistently used to maximize achievement of target LVOT-G and maintain LVEF \geq 50\%. For subjects with oHCM in the Phase 2 study (CY 6021), three opportunities for dose titration were provided (i.e., Weeks 2, 4, and 6). Dose titration proceeded until the reduction of the Valsalva LVOT-G to &lt; 50 mm Hg and resting LVOT-G to &lt; 30 mm Hg. Doses were adjusted to the maximum protocol-specified dose if LVEF \geq 50\%. If LVEF was &lt; 50\% at any time, the subjects was returned to the prior dose level or placed on placebo if the subjects was on the lowest dose level (i.e., 5 mg in Cohort 1 and 10 mg in Cohort 2).
L128: The Phase 3 trial (CY 6031) followed a similar dose titration strategy as the Phase 2 study (CY 6021); however, the LVOT-G target criteria for dose titration was simplified by focusing on the Valsalva LVOT-G only. The Valsalva LVOT-G target was reduced from 50 mm Hg to 30 mm Hg to maximize the potential therapeutic benefit of aficamten. In addition, the lower limit of LVEF for dose escalation was increased from 50% to 55% to provide a safety margin from the threshold of LVEF (&lt; 50\%) that triggered dose reduction in both studies. If LVEF was &lt; 50\% at any time, the subjects was returned to the prior dose level, or to placebo if the subject was on 5 mg. Subjects were able to receive up to 4 increasing dose levels (i.e., 5, 10, 15, and 20 mg) of aficamten over 3 dose titration opportunities. Doses of aficamten were individually titrated at Weeks 2, 4, and 6 using echocardiography, as outlined in Table 9. Dose escalation occurred only if a subject had a Valsalva LVOT-G \geq 30 mm Hg and an LVEF \geq 55\%. Week 8 represented the start of the predefined maintenance phase during which subjects with oHCM received echocardiographic assessments every 4 weeks through the end of trial at Week 24. Starting from Week 8, the dose was down-titrated if the LVEF was &lt; 50\%. If LVEF was &lt; 50\% at any time, the subject was returned to the prior dose level, or placed on placebo if the subject was on 5 mg.
L129: Dosing titration was implemented using an interactive Wed Response System (IWRS) in CY 6031. A sonographer and cardiologist (both blinded to randomized treatment assignment) performed and interpreted the echocardiogram and entered the results into the IWRS for dose titrations and adjustments. Investigational product (IP) was dispensed at scheduled trial visits using IWRS. Therefore, the investigator and site staff remained blinded to the echocardiogram images and results as well as treatment group allocation and dosing.

L135: Proposed To-Be-Marketed Dose Titration Scheme and Echocardiographic Assessment Frequency
L136: The goal of the proposed to-be-marketed aficamten daily dosing regimen is to maintain the favorable benefit-risk profile established in the Phase 3 trial (CY 6031), while allowing for more flexible clinic visits for subjects with oHCM. Therefore, the Applicant proposed visits of every two to eight weeks during the dose titration phase and every three to six months during the maintenance phase for echocardiographic assessments based on additional modeling and simulations (See Section 14.5).
L137: Table 10 provides a tabulated version of the proposed dosing algorithm. The echocardiography-based dose titration criteria (Rows 1 and 3 to 5) are identical to that employed in the Phase 3 trial (Table 9) with the exception of the recommendation for subjects on the highest aficamten dose (i.e., 20 mg) who still demonstrate LVEF ≥55% and LVOT-G ≥30 mm Hg to move to the maintenance phase (Table 10, Row 2). Proposed guidance on the resumption of aficamten therapy in subjects with oHCM after dose interruption due to an LVEF &lt;50% is also provided (Table 10, Rows 6 to 8).

L143: The Applicant’s proposed echocardiographic assessments during titration and maintenance appeared reasonable based on the population PK/PD modeling and simulation:
L144: 1. Assessment during titration phase: The Applicant’s proposed flexible echocardiographic assessment every 2 to 8 weeks (instead of at Weeks 2, 4, and 6 that was done in the Phase 3 trial) after initiation of treatment appeared reasonable. The pharmacometrics reviewer evaluated the frequency of echocardiographic assessment during the titration phase by performing model-based simulations at different titration frequencies of echocardiographic assessments ranging from every 2 weeks to every 8 weeks. Figure 2 and Table 11 summarize the percent of subjects with Valsalva LVOT-G &lt;30 mm Hg and LVEF &lt;50% based on various echocardiographic assessment frequencies during the titration phase. Briefly, from a safety perspective, every 2-week echocardiographic assessment (orange color) and every 8-week echocardiographic assessment (yellow color) provided similar percentages of subjects with LVEF &lt;50% at 24-weeks. For efficacy, compared to every 2-week echocardiographic assessment, every 8-week echocardiographic assessment resulted in 7% fewer subjects with LVOT-G &lt;30 mm Hg at 24 weeks. Beyond 24 weeks, a similar number of subjects achieved the titration goals for LVEF ≥50% or Valsalva LVOT-G &lt;30 mm Hg irrespective of titration frequencies.

L155: Assessment during maintenance phase: In the Phase 3 trial, echocardiographic assessments were performed every 4 weeks during the maintenance phase regardless of LVEF. For the to-be-marketed regimen, the Applicant proposed echocardiographic assessments every 3 to 6 months for subjects in the maintenance phase, and to increase the frequency of echocardiographic assessments to every 3 months for LVEF &lt;55% to \geq50%. The Applicant's proposal of assessing LVEF and Valsalva LVOT-G every 3 to 6 months in subjects during the maintenance phase appears reasonable. Figure 3 shows that the plasma levels of aficamten remained stable over 6 months with low inter- and intra-subject variability (%CV: 33.9% and 20.5% respectively) during the maintenance phase of the Phase 3 trial, CY 6031, with seven subjects having LVEF &lt;50%. Thus, PK variability over time is modest and contributes to the low within-subject variability of LVEF in the maintenance phase. Considering there were seven subjects showing LVEF &lt;50%, correlation analyses were performed to identify the predictor (aficamten peak concentration or prior LVEF) for LVEF &lt;50% using the data from Trial CY 6031. The results suggested that prior LVEF has better association with subsequent LVEF as compared to peak plasma concentration during the maintenance phase (Figure 4). The risk of LVEF &lt;50% was modestly higher (around 50%) in Trial CY 6031 if the preceding LVEF was less than 55%. Considering LVEF &lt;55% as a predictor of subsequent LVEF &lt;50%, correlation analyses were performed between LVEF at Week 24 (LVEFwk24) and preceding LVEFs at various timepoints including Week 20 (4-week preceding LVEFwk24), Week 16 (8-week preceding LVEFwk24), Week 12 (12-week preceding LVEFwk24), and Week 8 (16-week preceding LVEFwk24) using Trial CY 6031 data to identify the optimal assessment time for subjects with LVEF &lt;55% to \geq50% (Figure 5). The results suggested similar associations between prior LVEFs (i.e., 4-week preceding
L156: LVEFwk24, 8-week preceding LVEFwk24, and 12-week preceding LVEFwk24) and subsequent LVEF at Week 24, while weaker association was observed between LVEF at Week 8 (i.e., 16-week preceding LVEFwk24) and LVEF at Week 24. Thus, the Applicant’s proposed increased echocardiographic assessment to every 3 months is reasonable for subjects with LVEF &lt;55% to ≥50%.
L157: Figure 3. Plasma Levels of Aficamten During the Maintenance Phase, Trial CY 6031

L172: 6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To Demonstrate Efficacy
L173: 6.2.1. CY 6031 (SEQUOIA-HCM)
L174: The primary assessment of efficacy of aficamten for the treatment of adults with symptomatic oHCM was based on the results of the pivotal Phase 3 Trial CY 6031 (SEQUOIA-HCM).
L175: 6.2.1.1. Study Design, CY 6031
L176: SEQUOIA-HCM (CY 6031) was a multi-center, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial in subjects with symptomatic oHCM. Eligible subjects were randomized in a 1:1 ratio to receive aficamten or placebo in addition to background oHCM therapy for 24 weeks. Randomization was stratified by use of beta-blockers (capped at 70% for beta-blocker users) and CPET exercise modality (treadmill or bicycle). Other enrollment limits included disopyramide users that were capped at 10% of total enrollment and subjects with persistent atrial fibrillation (AF) at screening that were capped at 15% of total enrollment. Patients treated with
L177: disopyramide represent a small and specific subgroup of oHCM patients who are symptomatic despite standard of care. The pre-specified enrollment caps minimize the potential confounding factors due to potential differences in patient characteristics and ensure generalizability of the trial results.
L178: Investigational product was administered orally once daily with or without food. The starting dose of aficamten was 5 mg. Doses were individually titrated at Weeks 2, 4, and 6 based on echocardiography-guided criteria (Table 12). Dose escalation with 5-mg increment at Weeks 2, 4, and 6 occurred only if a subject had a Valsalva LVOT-G \geq 30 mm Hg and LVEF \geq 55\% (available doses: 5 mg, 10 mg, 15 mg, and 20 mg). At any visit, the dose was down-titrated if the LVEF was &lt; 50\% (40% to 49%). If a subject's LVEF was &lt; 40\% at any time, IP would be interrupted and held for at least 7 days. Repeat echocardiograms would be performed, and a subject could be restarted on IP after down-titration when a normal LVEF (\geq 55\%) was documented.
L179: Table 12. Echocardiogram Criteria for Scheduled Dose Titrations, Trial CY 6031
L180: Biplane LVEFValsalva LVOT-GAction



&lt; 50\%Reduce Dosea

\geq 50\% - 55\%No Dose Change

\geq 55\%and&lt; 30 mmHgNo Dose Change

\geq 55\%and\geq 30 mmHgIncrease Dose
L181: IP = investigational product; LVEF = left ventricular ejection fraction; LVOT-G = left ventricular outflow tract gradient
L182: a Once a participant's IP dose was down titrated, no further escalation was permitted. If LVEF was &lt; 50\% on 5 mg, the participant received placebo.
L183: Source: CY 6031 Clinical Study Report, Table 2
L184: The double-blind placebo-controlled treatment period lasted 24 weeks. Following the last dose of IP, there was a 4-week safety follow-up period.
L185: Figure 6. Trial Design, Trial CY 6031
L186: Figure 6: Trial Design, Trial CY 6031. The diagram shows the flow of patients through the study: Patients with oHCM treated with standard of care having a resting LVOT-G ≥ 30 mmHg, post-Valsalva peak LVOT-G ≥ 50 mmHg and NYHA Class II/III → Screening → Randomization → CK-3773274 + SoC (blue box) or Placebo + SoC (yellow box) → End of Study. Below this is a timeline of study visits: Screen, D1, W2, W4, W6, W8, W12, W16, W20, W24, W28. Rows indicate activities: Echocardiogram (up arrows at all visits), CPET (up arrows at all visits), KCCQ (up arrows from D1 onwards), NYHA (up arrows from D1 onwards), and Dose Titration (up arrows at W2, W4, W6).
L187: Source: CY 6031 protocol, Figure 2
L188: Abbreviations: CPET, cardiopulmonary exercise testing; KCCQ, Kansas City Cardiomyopathy Questionnaire; LVOT-G, left ventricular outflow tract gradient; NYHA, New York Heart Association; oHCM, obstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy; SoC, standard of care
L189: 6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria, CY 6031
L190: Key Inclusion Criteria
L191: • Between 18 and 85 years old at screening.• Body mass index &lt;35 \text{ kg/m}^2 at screening.• Acoustic windows to enable accurate transthoracic echocardiograms.• Diagnosed with HCM per the following criteria:– Had LV hypertrophy with nondilated LV chambers in the absence of other cardiac causes– Had an end-diastolic LV wall thickness \geq 15 \text{ mm} (or \geq 13 \text{ mm} with a known disease-causing gene mutation or positive family history of HCM) as determined by core laboratory interpretation, and– Had resting LVOT-G \geq 30 \text{ mm Hg} and Valsalva LVOT-G \geq 50 \text{ mm Hg} at screening.• LVEF \geq 60\% at screening as determined by the echocardiography core laboratory.• NYHA Functional Class II or III at screening.• Hemoglobin \geq 10 \text{ g/dL} at screening.• Respiratory exchange ratio \geq 1.05 and \text{pVO}_2 \leq 90\% at screening.• Subjects on beta-blockers, verapamil, diltiazem, or disopyramide should have been on a stable regimen for &gt;6 weeks prior to randomization and anticipate remaining on the same medication regimen during the trial. Subjects treated with disopyramide must also be treated concomitantly with a beta blocker and/or calcium channel blocker.
L192: Key Exclusion Criteria
L193: • Significant valvular heart disease:– Moderate-severe valvular aortic stenosis and/or regurgitation.– Moderate-severe mitral regurgitation not due to systolic anterior motion of the mitral valve.• Documented history of current obstructive coronary artery disease or myocardial infarction.• Known or suspected infiltrative, genetic or storage disorder causing cardiac hypertrophy that mimics oHCM.• Prior treatment with cardiotoxic agents such as doxorubicin.• History of LV systolic dysfunction (LVEF &lt;45\%) or stress cardiomyopathy at any time during their clinical course.• Had any electrocardiogram abnormality considered by the investigator to pose a risk to subject safety (e.g., second degree atrioventricular block type II).• Documented paroxysmal AF during the screening period.
L194: • Paroxysmal or permanent AF is only excluded if:– Rhythm restoring treatment has been required \leq 6 months prior to screening.– Rate control and anticoagulation has not been achieved at least 6 months prior to screening.• History of syncope or sustained ventricular tachyarrhythmia with exercise within 6 months prior to screening.• Implantable cardioverter-defibrillator (ICD) placement within 3 months prior to screening or planned ICD placement during the trial.• History of appropriate ICD discharge for life-threatening ventricular arrhythmia within 6 months prior to screening.• Had been treated with SRT or cannot postpone plans for SRT until after the trial period• Inability to exercise on a treadmill or bicycle.• Documented room air oxygen saturation reading &lt; 90\% at screening.• Had received prior treatment with aficamten or mavacamten.
L195: 6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, CY 6031
L196: The review division provided comments on the Trial CY 6031 protocol (preliminary meeting minutes dated June 1, 2021, End-of-Phase 2 meeting minutes dated August 3, 2021) and the statistical analysis plan (SAP) (dated August 11, 2023, and November 1, 2023).
L197: Sample Size
L198: The Applicant specified a sample size of 270 subjects, randomized 1:1 to aficamten or placebo, providing &gt; 90\% power to detect a change from baseline in pVO_2 to Week 24 of 1.5 mL/kg/min compared to placebo (2-sided type I error of 0.05), assuming a standard deviation of 3.5 mL/kg/min, and controlling for limiting beta-blocker use (less than \sim 70\%), limiting exercise modality of bicycle (less than \sim 50\%), and 10% of subjects with missing data (i.e., change from baseline data of the primary endpoint).
L199: During the trial, the Applicant pre-specified periodic assessments of the blinded aggregate pooled missing data rate and overall pooled SD for the change from baseline in pVO_2 at Week 24. Based on these assessments, the Applicant could increase the sample size once to maintain the intended power. Thus, there were 282 subjects, rather than 270, enrolled and randomized in the trial.
L200: Efficacy Endpoints
L201: Primary
L202: The primary endpoint of the trial was Change in pVO_2 by CPET from baseline to Week 24.
L203: Key Secondary
L204: • Change in KCCQ-CSS from baseline to Week 12 and Week 24.• Proportion of subjects with \geq 1 class improvement in NYHA Functional Class from baseline to Week 12 and Week 24.• Change in Valsalva LVOT-G from baseline to Week 12 and Week 24.• Proportion of subjects with Valsalva LVOT-G &lt; 30 mm Hg at Week 12 and Week 24.• Total duration of SRT eligibility during the 24-week treatment period in subjects who were SRT eligible at baseline.• Change in total workload during CPET from baseline to Week 24.
L205: Primary Analysis Population
L206: Primary efficacy analyses were performed on the full analysis set (FAS), which included all randomized subjects.
L207: Efficacy Analyses
L208: Efficacy analyses were performed in the FAS by the randomized treatment group. All hypothesis tests were reported as 2-sided p-values. To preserve an overall type I error rate of 0.05, testing of the primary and secondary endpoints followed a specified order using a closed testing procedure as described below.
L209: Null and Alternative Hypotheses
L210: The null hypothesis for the primary endpoint was that the treatment difference (aficamten minus placebo) of the mean change from baseline of pVO2 at Week 24 was 0.
L211: Multiple Comparisons and Multiplicity
L212: A closed testing procedure was used to address multiplicity and preserve the overall type I error rate. The primary endpoint was tested first at a two-sided 0.05 alpha threshold. If the primary endpoint achieved statistical significance, secondary endpoints were then tested in sequential order at the two-sided 0.05 level:
L213: • KCCQ-CSS change from baseline at Week 24.• Proportion of subjects with \geq 1 NYHA functional class improvement at Week 24.• Valsalva LVOT-G change from baseline at Week 24.• Proportion of subjects with Valsalva LVOT-G &lt; 30 mm Hg at Week 24.• Duration of SRT eligibility up to 24 weeks of treatment for those who were SRT eligible at baseline.• KCCQ-CSS change from baseline at Week 12.• Proportion of subjects with \geq 1 NYHA functional class improvement at Week 12.• Valsalva LVOT-G change from baseline at Week 12.
L214: • Proportion of subjects with Valsalva LVOT-G &lt;30 mm Hg at Week 12.• Change from baseline in total workload at Week 24.
L215: Primary Endpoint Analysis
L216: The primary analysis was performed using an analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) model that included terms of treatment, randomization stratification factors (beta-blocker use status and CPET modality), baseline pVO2 and baseline body weight as covariates in the FAS. Data analyzed include all observed Week 24 pVO2 values from subjects who completed 24 weeks of treatment, as well as from subjects who early terminate from the treatment but remain in the trial with Week 24 pVO2 measurements. Data were imputed for subjects who were missing Week 24 pVO2. Missing data were imputed using multiple imputation methodology under the missing at random (MAR) assumption for the primary analysis. One hundred imputed MAR datasets were generated. Results were combined using Rubin's rule to produce an LS mean estimate of the treatment difference, its 95% confidence interval (CI), and p-value (p). Additional statistical details are provided in Section 16).
L217: Secondary Endpoints Analysis
L218: Change in KCCQ-CSS and change in Valsalva LVOT-G from baseline to Week 12 and Week 24 were analyzed using a mixed model for repeated measures (MMRM) model with baseline as a covariate, randomization stratification factors, visit, treatment group, and interaction terms of treatment by visit and baseline by visit. Since there was a &gt;10% difference in baseline KCCQ-CSS across regions, treatment by visit and region and baseline by visit and region were also included in the model. The treatment effect at each visit week was estimated from the interaction terms of treatment by visit by region, with coefficients for each region determined by proportion of subjects evaluated in each region. An unstructured covariance matrix was specified. All data observed up to Week 24 post-randomization was included in the model. The LS mean estimates, differences, 95% CIs, and p-values were reported for the results.
L219: The proportion of subjects with \geq 1 class improvement in NYHA Functional Class and proportion of subjects with Valsalva LVOT-G &lt;30 mm Hg at Week 12 and Week 24 were analyzed using the Cochran–Mantel–Haenszel test stratified by randomization factors. For the proportion of subjects with \geq 1 class improvement in NYHA class at Week 12, Week 16 NYHA class was used if Week 12 NYHA class was not available. Week 8 NYHA class was used if Week 16 NYHA was not available essentially making this a closest observation imputation. Subject was considered as not achieving \geq 1 improvement in NYHA class at Week 12 if no NYHA at Weeks 8, 12, and 16 was available. Similarly, Week 20 NYHA class was used if Week 24 NYHA was not available. Subjects were considered as not achieving \geq 1 improvement in NYHA class at Week 24 if Week 20 and Week 24 NYHA were not available.
L220: For the proportion of subjects with post-Valsalva LVOT &lt;30 mm Hg at Week 12, Week 16 post-Valsalva LVOT was used if the Week 12 visit was performed but post-Valsalva LVOT was not available. Week 8 post-Valsalva LVOT was used if Week 16 was not available. Similarly, Week 20 post Valsalva LVOT was used if Week 24 was performed but post Valsalva LVOT was not available. The p-value and 95% CI were obtained using an exact method.
L221: Total duration of SRT eligibility was calculated as the number of days subjects are SRT eligible, from the date of the randomization until Week 24. Because the SRT eligibility status of a subject
L222: may change during the trial conduct, only intervals during which the subject is SRT eligible, were summed. Total duration of SRT eligibility during the 24-week of treatment period was analyzed using an ANCOVA model that includes treatment and randomization stratification factor beta blocker use/nonuse as fixed effects, and significant baseline characteristics as covariates. Significance of baseline covariates was evaluated using a stepwise model selection method with a default stay or entry level of 0.05.
L223: Change in total workload during CPET from baseline to Week 24 was analyzed using the same analysis approach as for the primary endpoint analysis.
L224: It should be noted that the review team did not agree with the Applicant's approach for missing data. For the primary analyses, the Applicant imputed the missing data under the missing-at-random assumption despite the advice provided by the biometrics division, dated August 11, 2023, on the CY 6031 statistical analysis plan (SAP):
L225: We continue to disagree that an MAR assumption is appropriate given that patients with missing data are likely to be different from those who remain in the trial on treatment. It is generally not acceptable because patients who drop out of the study and are no longer on study treatment are not expected to behave in the same way as those who continue in the study on treatment, which would not be in line with a treatment policy estimand, and those who die should not be imputed with similar outcomes to those who are able to remain in the study. You can use different imputation models based on the type of intercurrent events which are the reasons for missing data. For example, retrieved dropouts (those who discontinue treatment but continue study visits) can be used to impute data for those who dropout of the study. For those who die before the primary endpoint can be assessed, we generally accept a worst-case type of imputation where the worst 10% of observed outcomes can be used for a multiple imputation of missing data due to death.
L226: Additionally, the review team did not agree with the use of stepwise model selection approach due to well-known issues with overestimation of model fit and increase in type I error. In this case, no adjusting covariates were ultimately included in the model.
L227: Subgroup Analysis of the Primary Endpoint
L228: Subgroup analyses for the primary efficacy endpoint were performed using the following variables:
L229: • Sex (male, female)• Age group (&lt;65, ≥65 years old)• Baseline body mass index (&lt;30 versus ≥30)• Baseline NYHA Class (II, III)• Baseline KCCQ-CSS (≤ median and &gt; median)• Baseline LVEF (≤ median and &gt; median)• Baseline NT-proBNP (≤ median and &gt; median)• CPET modality (treadmill, bicycle)
L230: NDA 219083MYQORZO (aficamten)
L231: • Baseline pVO2 (≤ median, &gt; median)• Beta Blocker (use, no use)• Baseline resting LVOT (≤ median and &gt; median)• Sarcomeric gene mutation status
L232: An information request was submitted on November 14, 2024, to perform subgroup analysis also by race and ethnicity. The Applicant provided these analyses, and these results were subsequently incorporated into the review.
L233: Protocol Amendments
L234: The original protocol dated July 26, 2021, was amended four times. In the NDA submission, the Applicant provided the following milestone dates:
L235: • First subject enrolled: February 1, 2022• Last subject completed: December 19, 2023• Protocol version and date: Amendment 4 dated December 8, 2023• SAP version and date: Final (Version 02) October 2023
L236: A summary of protocol amendments is given in Section 16.
L237: 6.2.1.4. Results of Analyses, CY 6031
L238: Subject Disposition
L239: A total of 543 persons were screened for the trial. Of these, 261 failed screening, primarily for not satisfying the inclusion/exclusion criteria (98.9%) as shown in Table 13.

L243: The remaining screened subjects (n=282) were enrolled and randomized to treatment (142 to aficamten and 140 to placebo). Table 14 shows the disposition of all the randomized subjects. Of the subjects randomized to aficamten, five (3.5%) discontinued treatment early: two due to subject withdrawal, two due to a protocol deviation, and one due to an adverse event (AE). Of the subjects randomized to placebo, four (2.9%) discontinued treatment early: two due to an AE, one due to physician decision, and one due to other reason. All subjects in both treatment groups received at least one dose of IP, so the All-Randomized set matched the primary FAS.

L265: Baseline Characteristics
L266: Table 17 examines the between-group balance on baseline clinical characteristics in the FAS population. At baseline, subjects had a median body mass index of 28.1 kg/m2. A total of 61 subjects (32 aficamten, 29 placebo) were SRT eligible at baseline. The mean pVO2 by CPET at baseline was 18.5 mL/kg/min, with 55% of subjects using treadmill as the CPET modality. The baseline clinical characteristics were generally balanced between the aficamten and placebo treatment groups.

L289: Because FDA did not agree to the MAR assumption made in the primary analysis, the review team conducted an additional analysis imputing missing data from subjects who discontinued
L290: aficamten using placebo-based multiple imputation. There were not a sufficient number of retrieved dropouts to perform a retrieved dropouts multiple imputation. Missing data for subjects who remained on aficamten treatment were imputed assuming MAR based on the model derived from observed data within the aficamten group. Results from this analysis, 1.69 mL/kg/min (95% CI: 0.99, 2.39; p &lt; 0.0001), were slightly attenuated, but similar to the primary MAR analysis as shown in Table 18.
L291: Sensitivity analyses for the primary endpoint are presented in Section 16. Results are generally consistent with the primary analysis.
L292: Primary Endpoint Subgroup Analysis Results
L293: Subgroup analyses were conducted to assess the potential for differences in the treatment effect for various demographic groups and are presented in Section 16. Overall, the treatment effect of aficamten when compared to placebo was generally consistent with the overall population across demographic subgroups of age, sex, race, and ethnicity. Note that ethnic and racial minorities were not well-represented in this trial.
L294: Secondary Endpoints Analyses Results
L295: All secondary endpoints were found to be statistically significant for both the Applicant's specified methodology, as well as the additional FDA analyses (Table 20, Table 21, Table 22, Table 23, Table 24, and Table 25).
L296: KCCQ-CSS
L297: The mean baseline KCCQ-CSS score was approximately 75 (scores range from 0-100, with higher scores indicating better health status). The estimated KCCQ-CSS change from baseline at Week 24 was 11.6 points for aficamten and 4.3 points for placebo with an estimated LS mean difference of 7.3 points (95% CI: 4.6, 10.1; p &lt; 0.0001). For change from baseline at Week 12, estimates were 11.1 points for aficamten and 4.0 points for placebo. The LS mean difference was 7.0 points (95% CI: 4.5, 9.5; p &lt; 0.0001). Seven subjects had missing KCCQ-CSS (4 in aficamten and 3 in placebo) data at Week 24. Missing data were handled as MAR by the MMRM model in the prespecified analysis.
L298: The statistical reviewer performed an additional analysis where intermittent missing data were imputed using the Markov Chain Monte Carlo method under MAR assumption. After the first step of imputation, the monotone missing data were imputed using placebo-based imputations. Imputed datasets were analyzed using an MMRM model. The LS mean difference between treatment groups at Week 12 was 7.0 points (95% CI: 4.6, 9.5; p &lt; 0.0001) and at Week 24 was 7.3 points (95% CI: 4.2, 10.0; p &lt; 0.0001), essentially unchanged from the primary analysis method.

L305: An information request was submitted on December 18, 2024, and December 23, 2024, regarding the clinical meaningfulness of the KCCQ-CSS instrument and datasets with variables indicating the values imputed for missing data. The Clinical Outcome Assessment (COA) team was consulted to assess clinical meaningfulness of the KCCQ-CSS results and the proposed labeling claim (COA review dated October 10, 2025). The COA team concluded that KCCQ-CSS was fit-for-purpose to support its context of use in subjects with oHCM; and the Applicant's proposed threshold of 10 points as a reflection of KCCQ-CSS within-subject clinically meaningful change from baseline to Week 24 appeared reasonable. The COA team further concluded that the evidence provided by the Applicant generally supported the proposed labeling claims.

L317: At baseline, the mean Valsalva LVOT was approximately 83 mm Hg. Similarly, at Week 24, the LS mean change from baseline in Valsalva LVOT-G showed a statistically significant improvement in the aficamten group compared with the placebo group with LS mean changes of -48.1 mm Hg and +2.2 mm Hg, respectively, with a treatment difference in aficamten versus placebo of -50.3 mm Hg (95% CI: -56.9, -43.7; p &lt; 0.0001). L318: missing values at Week 12 and 11 (3.9%) had missing values at Week 24. The missing values were handled as MAR by an MMRM model.

L325: The trial required subjects to have a resting LVOT-G \geq 30 mm Hg at screening. L327: There were 6 subjects with missing values at Week 12, of which 2 were imputed using values from other weeks. These subjects were considered as not achieving Valsalva LVOT-G &lt;30 mm Hg per core laboratory determination.

Results were similar with an odds ratio (versus placebo) at Week 12 of 17.8 (95% CI: 8.1, 39.2; p &lt; 0.0001) and at Week 24 of 23.6 (95% CI: 9.1, 61.4; p &lt; 0.0001).

L336: Duration of SRT Eligibility
L337: SRT eligibility was defined as a subject being classified as NYHA class \geq 3 and with resting or Valsalva LVOT-G \geq 50 mm Hg. At baseline, 61 (22%) subjects (32 in the aficamten group and 29 in the placebo group) were eligible for SRT. During the 24-week treatment period, aficamten treatment resulted in a significant reduction in the time spent as SRT eligible by an LS mean of -78.1 days (95% CI: -99.8, -56.3; p &lt; 0.0001) compared with placebo. There were no missing values. The ANCOVA model included treatment and randomization stratification factor beta blocker use/nonuse as fixed effects. No baseline characteristics were included in the model.

L351: 6.2.2. CY 6021 (REDWOOD-HCM)
L352: The REDWOOD-HCM study was a Phase 2, multi-center, randomized, placebo-controlled, dose-finding study to evaluate the safety, tolerability, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of aficamten in adults with symptomatic HCM. The study consisted of 4 cohorts with treatment duration of 10 weeks with a 4-week follow-up period after the last dose. Cohort 1 consisted of subjects with oHCM not receiving disopyramide who were randomized 2:1 (n=14; n=8) to receive up to 3 escalating doses of aficamten (5 mg, 10 mg, and 15 mg once daily) or placebo. Cohort 2 (n=14 aficamten, n=6 placebo) was a replica of Cohort 1 except for the 3 escalating doses of aficamten (10 mg, 20 mg, 30 mg once daily). Cohort 3 consisted of subjects with oHCM whose background HCM therapy included disopyramide. All subjects in Cohort 3 (n=13) received up to 3 escalating doses of aficamten (5, 10, and 15 mg) based on echocardiographic guidance. Cohort 4 consisted of subjects (n=41) with nHCM whose background therapy included beta-blockers and/or calcium channel blockers, either as monotherapy or in combination therapy. Subjects receiving disopyramide were excluded from Cohort 4. Subjects in Cohort 4 received up to 3 doses of aficamten (5, 10, and 15 mg).
L353: Efficacy was evaluated by LVOT-G, NYHA classification, and subject-reported outcome quality of life assessment. There were no adjustments for multiplicity. For subjects in Cohort 1 and 2, there were statistically significant reductions in LVOT-G, both resting and post-Valsalva, from baseline compared to pooled placebo at Week 10:
L354: • Cohort 1:– Resting LVOT-G: -41 mm Hg aficamten; -15 mm Hg placebo (nominal p=0.0009)– Post-Valsalva LVOT-G: -40 mm Hg aficamten; -5 mm Hg placebo (nominal p=0.0005)• Cohort 2:– Resting LVOT-G: -40 mm Hg aficamten; -15 mm Hg pooled placebo (nominal p=0.001)– Post-Valsalva LVOT-G: -51 mm Hg aficamten; -5 mm Hg pooled placebo (nominal p&lt;0.0001)
L355: Improvements of at least one NYHA functional class from baseline to Week 10 were observed in 43% and 64% of subjects treated with aficamten in Cohorts 1 and 2, respectively, compared to 33% of the pooled placebo-treated subjects (see Section 16.2).
L356: Cohorts 3 and 4 did not have respective control groups. Further, subjects in Cohort 4 had nonobstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy for which an LVOT-G evaluation was not possible. Thus, data interpretation was challenging in Cohorts 3 and 4 and, therefore, not considered to be confirmatory evidence.
L357: In summary, the REDWOOD-HCM study demonstrated sizeable PD effects on LVOT-G when added to standard-of-care. These findings reasonably confirm/support the results from the pivotal SEQUOIA-HCM trial.
L358: 6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L359: 6.3.1. Is the Observed Treatment Effect of Aficamten on pVO2 Clinically Meaningful?
L360: Issue
L361: Baseline pVO2 was approximately 18 mL/kg/min. The primary endpoint of change in pVO2 from baseline to Week 24 was statistically significant (p&lt;0.0001) with a mean difference of 1.7 mL/kg/min between treatment groups. The clinical meaningfulness of the result needs to be established.
L362: Background
L363: In HCM, pVO2 is associated with clinical prognosis and death (Masri et al. 2015); however, the relationship between a change in pVO2 and clinical outcomes is not extensively studied or well defined. An increase of 1 mL/kg/min in pVO2 was found to be predictive of reduced risk of death from HF and need for heart transplantation in patients with HCM (Coats et al. 2015). However, this study was a retrospective observational study from a single center, and the generalizability of the study findings is uncertain. In patients with HF and reduced ejection fraction, a small increase in pVO2 (~1 mL/kg/min) was associated with a 5% lower risk of all-cause mortality or
L364: all-cause hospitalization (p &lt; 0.001), a 4% lower risk of cardiovascular mortality or cardiovascular hospitalization, and a 7% lower risk of all-cause mortality in a randomized-controlled trial (Swank et al. 2012). However, it is uncertain whether the described association of pVO_2 with cardiovascular outcomes in patients with HF with reduced ejection fraction (HFrEF) with LVEF \leq 35\% can be extrapolated to the HCM population with preserved LV systolic function and patients who have a reasonably normal life expectancy overall. Current evidence is insufficient to quantify the magnitude of clinical benefit associated with a small increase in pVO_2 in with HCM. Despite the uncertainties, it should be noted that improvement with aficamten treatment was observed across a broader range of clinical endpoints including statistical and clinical significance in KCCQ-CSS and NYHA Class. Other supporting efficacy results include large, statistically significant findings in LVOT-G reduction and improvement in total workload from the CPET. The observed consistent results clearly establish that aficamten improves how patients with oHCM function and feel. Additional analyses to demonstrate the clinical significance of the observed pVO_2 changes are described below.
L365: Assessment
L366: Clinical Outcomes Assessment Consult Review
L367: The Applicant did not propose a range of pVO_2 change that is clinically meaningful to patients. COA was consulted during the NDA review for assistance in interpreting meaningful change in pVO_2 scores. Exploratory anchor-based analyses using NYHA, KCCQ-Physical Limitation Score, and Patient Global Impressions of Change have been conducted. However, given the low correlation coefficients (i.e., |r| &lt; 0.3) between pVO_2 and the anchor scales for the changes from baseline at Week 24, a suitable anchor scale to estimate pVO_2 meaningful change in CY 6031 was not identified.
L368: While the threshold for within-subject clinically meaningful change from baseline at Week 24 in pVO_2 could not be estimated, the cumulative distribution function curves show a clear separation between groups across a wide range of pVO_2 improvement change scores in CY 6031.
L369: Figure 7. Cumulative Distribution of Change From Baseline to Week 24 in pVO_2, Full Analysis Set, Trial CY 6031

L372: Abbreviation: pVO_2, peak oxygen consumption
L373: Exploratory Endpoint
L374: A composite endpoint of pVO2 and NYHA functional class was explored to further assess functional improvement defined as:
L375: • A pVO2 increase of \geq 1.5 mL/kg/min and a \geq 1 NYHA class improvement at Week 24, or
• A pVO2 increase of \geq 3.0 mL/kg/min and no worsening of NYHA class at Week 24
L376: A greater proportion of subjects in the aficamten group achieved this composite efficacy endpoint compared to the placebo group (42.3% versus 13.6%, respectively) (see Table 26).
