L379: Source: Statistical reviewer analysis
L380: Proportion of responders was analyzed using Cochran–Mantel–Haenszel test stratified by beta blocker use (Yes/No) and CPET modality. Responder is defined as change from baseline of \geq 1.5 mL/kg/min in pVO2 and \geq 1 class improvement in NYHA functional class or change from baseline of \geq 3.0 mL/kg/min in pVO2 and no worsening of NYHA functional class. Subjects with missing pVO2 or missing NYHA functional class are treated as nonresponders.
L381: Abbreviations: CI, confidence interval; CPET, cardiopulmonary exercise testing; N, number of subjects in treatment group; n, number of subjects with given characteristic; NYHA, New York Heart Association; pVO2, peak oxygen consumption
L382: The division considers that a change in NYHA Class of 1 or more represents a clinically meaningful improvement. Adding NYHA class as a component of the efficacy endpoint strengthens the validity of clinical response. It is also important to note that some subjects can have more sizeable improvements in pVO2, as evidenced for example, by the proportion of subjects with \geq 3.0 mL/kg/min improvement without worsening in NYHA class. Approximately 26% of aficamten-treated subjects met this component of the exploratory efficacy endpoint compared to 9% of subjects treated with placebo. Furthermore, this composite endpoint was previously used as a primary efficacy endpoint to support approval of mavacamten, the first-in-class, cardiac myosin inhibitor (NDA 214998) in the phase 3 EXPLORER-HCM trial. The division considered the improvement defined in this composite endpoint clinically meaningful. The proportion of subjects who achieved this composite endpoint in EXPLORER-HCM was 37% versus 17% in the mavacamten and placebo group, respectively with a difference of 19.4% and odds ratio of 2.7 (p = 0.0005). Change in pVO2 was also studied as a secondary endpoint in EXPLORER-HCM
L383: with a mean difference of 1.4 mL/kg/min. Overall, the aficamten treatment effects on the exploratory composite endpoint of pVO2 and NYHA functional class, as well as on the continuous pVO2, were similar to those observed with mavacamten.
L384: Conclusion
L385: The totality of data indicates that the observed changes in pVO2 in CY 6031 are both statistically and clinically significant.
L386: 6.3.2. Impact of Imputation of Missing Data for Continuous Variable Endpoints on Assessment of Efficacy
L387: Issue
L388: For the primary analyses of the primary and continuous secondary endpoints, the Applicant imputed all the missing data using multiple imputations under missing at random assumption regardless of reasons for the missing data.
L389: Background
L390: The source of missing data, particularly when linked to intercurrent events, plays a key role in determining how missing data should be handled. While missingness due to external, study-independent factors may be classified as MAR, this is uncommon. In most cases, missing data are associated with treatment adherence. It is generally not appropriate to impute missing data from non-adherent subjects using data from adherent subjects within the same treatment group, as differences in treatment exposure and behavior can lead to biased estimates of the treatment effect. We recommend that missing data from non-adherent subjects in the primary analyses be addressed using data from other non-adherent subjects within the same group who have available measurements at the specified analysis timepoint (retrieved dropouts). See Section 6.2.1.3.
L391: Assessment
L392: The statistical reviewer performed additional analyses where missing data for subjects who discontinued the treatment were imputed as missing not at random using placebo-based multiple imputation. There were not enough data to perform a retrieved dropouts' imputation. The results of these analyses are shown in Table 18, Table 20, Table 22, and Table 25.
L393: Conclusion
L394: The results of the additional analyses were generally similar to those seen in the Applicant's analyses and did not alter overall conclusions regarding efficacy. Results from the appropriate analyses that are generally recommended and implemented for these endpoints should be used for labeling purposes.
L395: 6.3.3. Impact of Imputation of Missing Data for Categorical Variable Endpoints on Assessment of Efficacy
L396: Issue
L397: For the categorical secondary endpoints, the Applicant imputed missing data using single imputation using measurements from other weeks; either last observation carried forward or next observation carried backward.
L398: Background
L399: The review division does not typically accept single imputation methods as this ignores uncertainty which will underestimate variability. Multiple imputation, such as a retrieved dropouts or jump-to reference, are typically preferred. Also, we do not agree with last observation carried forward or next observation carried backward imputation as it may introduce bias, misrepresent treatment effect, and underestimate variability by assuming that outcome remains constant over time. Although failure imputation is a single imputation method for responder analyses, such an analysis could be acceptable if the imputation was accounted for in the interpretation of the results with little missing data that is similar between groups (i.e., one group was not proportionally higher in missing data rates).
L400: Assessment
L401: The statistical reviewer performed additional analyses as shown in Table 21 and Table 23. For the NYHA functional class categorical endpoint, subjects with missing data were classified as non-responders in the analysis. For the categorical Valsalva LVOT-G endpoint (Table 23), missing values imputed for the analysis of the continuous Valsalva LVOT-G were categorized as responders and non-responders based on the imputed values prior to the analysis.
L402: Conclusion
L403: The results were generally similar to those seen in the Applicant's analyses and did not alter overall conclusions regarding efficacy. Results from the appropriate analyses that are generally recommended and implemented for these endpoints should be used for labeling purposes.
L404: 7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
L405: 7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L406: All toxicology species were pharmacologically relevant considering the evolutionary conservation of cardiac myosin across mammals. A brief discussion of clinically relevant toxicities is included below. Detailed summaries of the nonclinical studies are provided in Section 13.1.
L407: Exaggerated Pharmacology/Cardiac Toxicity
L408: Cardiac effects were, unsurprisingly, observed in repeat-dose toxicology studies conducted in rats and dogs at or near exposure levels anticipated for clinical use, which were interpreted as direct results of the intended pharmacology in healthy animals. A similar pattern was observed in the toxicology studies with mavacamten, the first-in-class cardiac myosin inhibitor.
L409: In healthy rats, the cardiac findings, which were interpreted as overpredicting clinical risk, consisted of dose-dependent increases in irreversible dilated cardiomyopathy, increased heart weight, ventricular wall thinning, and premature deaths at exposures ranging from approximately 4- and 11-times the clinical exposures based on free AUC and free Cmax, respectively, at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD). Continued treatment of healthy rats culminated in heart failure and deaths in a dose and duration-dependent fashion.
L410: In shorter studies in healthy dogs, at exposures exceeding the anticipated clinical exposures at the MRHD, aficamten caused dose-dependent cardiac effects including increased heart rate, ventricular dilatation, increased heart weight, and cardiomyocyte degeneration with necrosis, with high-dose animals showing persistent ventricular dilatation and increased heart weight (males only) even after a recovery period. In a longer 39-week study at lower doses (up to 6-times clinical exposure), aficamten was better tolerated with only reversible increases in heart rate and QTc interval, and transient heart weight increases in males that resolved by study termination.
L411: Effects Secondary to Cardiac Toxicity
L412: Inhibiting cardiac contractility in otherwise healthy animals led to secondary effects on various organ systems due to altered hemodynamics and compromised blood perfusion. These effects were particularly notable in the liver and the lung. Acute liver necrosis with elevated hepatic enzymes occurred in high-dose male rats secondary to congestive heart failure caused by aficamten-induced reduced cardiac contractility, with no hepatotoxicity observed at lower doses in rats or in dog studies. Pulmonary effects in rats included increased lung weight with hemosiderin-laden alveolar macrophages and lung hemorrhage in high-dose males, consistent with secondary lung edema due to heart failure associated with cardiac dilatation. Taken together, no unanticipated off-target toxicities were observed in healthy animals with repeat aficamten dosing. The clinical relevance of the findings was considered limited for the target patient population.
L413: Mutagenicity/Carcinogenicity
L414: Aficamten was not mutagenic or clastogenic in a standard battery of genotoxicity studies including the Ames assay and a combined in vivo micronucleus assay and comet assay.
L415: There were no aficamten-related tumor findings in a 26-week carcinogenicity study in transgenic rasH2 mice at doses up to 2.0 mg/kg/day or in a 2-year carcinogenicity study in Sprague Dawley rats at doses up to 3 mg/kg/day (corresponding to 4-times the exposure at the MRHD based on AUCunbound).
L416: Developmental Toxicity
L417: In embryofetal and pre- and postnatal development studies, when pregnant rats were administered aficamten during the period of organogenesis, aficamten increased the incidence of
L418: structural malformations at exposures \geq 4-times the clinical exposures at the MRHD of 20 mg based on AUCunbound. No adverse effects on development were seen at 3-times the clinical exposure at the MRHD. See Sections 8.4 and 13.1 for more details.
L419: 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L420: Aficamten is a cardiac myosin inhibitor. The primary risk associated with this class of drugs is the potential for development of signs or symptoms of HF due to an exaggerated pharmacologic effect resulting in systolic dysfunction (reduced LVEF). The risk is greater among subjects who concurrently take medications that inhibit multiple P450 pathways involved in aficamten elimination.
L421: 7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L422: Aficamten is not approved in the United States or in any foreign market.
L423: 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L424: FDA's safety evaluation focused on the data obtained in CY 6031 (SEQUOIA-HCM). This pivotal study provides controlled data to evaluate the safety profile of aficamten under echocardiography-based dosing and monitoring in oHCM subjects. As agreed, the Applicant provided a Summary of Clinical Safety that included safety data from the CY 6031, CY 6021 (dose-ranging, placebo-controlled Phase 2 study) and CY 6022 (ongoing open-label extension study) (see the overview of clinical trials in Table 3). Given the different dose groups and dosing strategies between studies, pooled controlled data from CY 6031 and CY 6021 were not performed. Data from CY 6021 were evaluated separately as supportive safety data. The ongoing extension Study CY 6022 provides long-term safety data in oHCM to evaluate safety topics of interest.
L425: The overall data and submission quality are reasonable. Adverse events were analyzed by use of the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA; Version 27.0) preferred term, and by pooling AEs with a similar medical concept (referred to as the Standardised MedDRA Query or Office of New Drugs Custom Medical Queries [OCMQs]). All AEs presented and discussed are treatment-emergent AEs unless specified otherwise. Treatment-emergent AEs are defined as any AE with an onset date on or after the first dose of study treatment, or with worsening on or after the first dose of study treatment up through 28 days after the last dose of study treatment (aficamten has a half-life of \sim 3.5 days).
L426: FDA's safety review focused on categorizing the on-target effects of aficamten on decreasing ejection fraction and increasing the risk of systolic cardiac failure. Clinical events of special interest included major adverse cardiac events, new-onset persistent AF and ventricular arrhythmias. Other safety evaluations included the analyses of exposure, AEs, echocardiographic data, laboratory data and vital signs.
L427: 7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
L428: The exposure was balanced in both treatment groups in CY 6031 with a mean exposure of 24 weeks. The ongoing extension Study CY 6022 provided an additional mean exposure of 62 weeks among 296 subjects with oHCM. A summary of exposure in CY 6031 and CY 6022 is shown in Table 27 and Table 28, respectively. The size of the safety database and duration of exposure are acceptable given the prevalence of the disease.

L439: 7.6. Safety Results
L440: 7.6.1. Safety Results
L441: 7.6.1.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
L442: The overall incidence of AEs and serious adverse events (SAEs) was generally similar between the treatment groups in CY 6031. In the aficamten group, there was one AE, compared to two in the placebo group, leading to discontinuation of study drug (see Section 7.6.1.4). Two AEs leading to treatment interruption in the aficamten group (same number in the placebo group) were cholecystitis and COVID-19; both events were transient and resolved, allowing the subjects to complete the treatment.

L451: 7.6.1.2. Deaths
L452: No deaths occurred in either treatment group in CY 6031. With the updated safety data through August 31, 2024, there were also no deaths reported in the ongoing extension Study CY 6022. One death due to cardiac arrest occurred in the dose-ranging Study CY 6021. This death involved a patient with nHCM and was deemed unlikely to be related to aficamten. A summary of this case is provided below:
L453: Subject CY-6021- (b) (6) : A 43-year-old Asian female subject with symptomatic nHCM received escalating doses of aficamten (5 mg, 10 mg, and 15 mg) over 45 days. On Day 45, the subject suffered a cardiac arrest while showering and died despite resuscitation efforts. The subject was receiving aficamten 15 mg. The investigator assessed the cardiac arrest as severe but not related to the study drug. This assessment was based on several factors: the subject's LVEF remained within the normal range throughout the study (73% at baseline and 75% at the Week 6 visit and during hospital admission on Day 45), global longitudinal strain improved, NT-proBNP and Troponin I levels improved compared to baseline. Additionally, the subject had a history of aborted sudden cardiac death, which was considered a likely cause of the cardiac arrest.
L454: 7.6.1.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
L455: Overall, the incidence of SAEs was similar between groups in CY 6031. No subject in the aficamten group discontinued treatment due to an SAE. No SAE was considered by the investigator to be related to study treatment. Narrative review of the cases in the aficamten group did not reveal major concerns. The three SAEs of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy in the aficamten group occurred after discontinuation of treatment during the safety follow-up period (range from 8-22 days after the last aficamten treatment).

L461: Serious adverse events defined as any untoward medical occurrence that at any dose results in death, is life-threatening, requires hospitalization or prolongation of existing hospitalization, results in persistent incapacity or substantial disruption of the ability to conduct normal life functions, or is a congenital anomaly or birth defect.
L462: Duration is 24 weeks.
L463: Risk difference (with 95% confidence interval) is shown between total treatment and comparator.
L464: Abbreviations: AE, adverse event; CI, confidence interval; incl, including; N, number of subjects in treatment group; n, number of subjects with adverse event; SAE, serious adverse event; SOC, system organ class
L465: 7.6.1.4. Adverse Events Leading to Treatment Discontinuation
L466: Study treatment was discontinued early due to an AE for one subject (0.7%) in the aficamten group and two subjects (1.4%) in the placebo group (Table 31). In the aficamten group, the AE that led to treatment discontinuation was mild paranoia, which was considered not related to aficamten.

L482: 7.6.1.6. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
L483: Hypertension was the most common AE reported in CY 6031. It was also the only AE that occurred at a frequency of \geq 5\% higher in the aficamten group compared to the placebo group (8% versus 2%) (Table 33). Evaluation of hypertension is discussed in Section 7.6.1.6.1.
L484: Palpitations was another common AE reported in CY 6031, occurring more frequently in subjects on aficamten than placebo (7% versus 3%). Palpitations are subjective symptoms that are common in patients with HCM. These symptoms are nonspecific and can be attributed to a wide range of causes and underlying diseases. The palpitation events observed in the aficamten group were transient and did not recur despite continued treatment. There was no apparent pattern in the onset of these events relative to the initiation of aficamten treatment (ranging from 4 to 360 days). Importantly, reports of palpitations were not associated with any clinically significant events, such as arrhythmia. Given these observations with a low incidence, a causal relationship between these palpitation events and aficamten treatment was unlikely.
L485: Heart failure related-events were reported in four aficamten-treated subjects (three cases reported as cardiac failure and one case reported as pulmonary edema) and one placebo-treated subject (cardiac failure congestive). Three of the events in the aficamten group occurred after discontinuation of treatment, during the safety follow-up period (range from two to eight days after last aficamten treatment). Given the long half-life of aficamten, a causal relationship between aficamten and these events cannot be excluded. The single on-treatment AE of cardiac failure (moderate intensity) was thought to be related to aficamten (see more details in Section 7.6.1.7).
L486: Numerical imbalance of back pain, paresthesia, renal and urinary tract infection, and rash was also observed in CY 6031; however, the frequency of these AEs was overall low, and the difference between groups was small (&lt;3%). These events were transient and generally reported to be mild in severity. Due to the nature of the events, the significance of these events in aficamten-treated subjects is unclear and these minor imbalances are likely due to chance.

L495: 7.6.1.6.1. Hypertension
L496: There was a small imbalance in hypertension AEs in Trial CY 6031; 11 (8%) on aficamten compared to 3 (2%) on placebo. The majority of the hypertension events in the aficamten group were mild (8 out of 11), with no severe cases or SAEs reported. None of the hypertension events led to treatment discontinuation, interruption or dose changes. Most subjects who experienced hypertension had reported a medical history of hypertension at baseline (8 out of 11). There was
L497: no apparent pattern of event onset relative to aficamten treatment initiation (the time to onset ranged from 15 to 173 days).
L498: A total of 15 subjects (7%) experienced hypertension AEs in the open-label extension Study CY 6022. The nature and severity of these events were consistent with the results in CY 6031. There was one SAE of hypertensive urgency that occurred in a 57-year-old female subject with oHCM and a history of essential hypertension. Their baseline blood pressure (BP) was elevated at 174/92 mm Hg. The SAE occurred 13 days after initiating aficamten treatment. The subject presented to the emergency room after experiencing significantly elevated BP of 214/99 mm Hg at home and was subsequently hospitalized due to the SAE. Aficamten (5 mg) was interrupted, and the event resolved after 2 days. The subject restarted aficamten and was subsequently up-titrated to 10 mg and 15 mg without any recurrence of the hypertensive event.
L499: Consistent with the AE findings, there was a small mean increase in both systolic and diastolic BP in the aficamten group compared to the placebo group (see Section 7.6.1.10). Aficamten-associated increases in BP are thought to be a consequence of relief of LVOT obstruction with improved cardiac output. The clinical review team recommends describing the hypertension AE findings and blood pressure changes in Section 6 of the labeling.
L500: 7.6.1.7. Echocardiograms: LVEF
L501: Consistent with the aficamten mechanism of action, there was a small mean decrease in LVEF (~4 to 5% with an SD of 7%) during 24 weeks of aficamten treatment compared with placebo (Figure 8). Mean LVEF returned toward baseline at Week 28, following the 4-week safety follow-up period.

L504: Source: adlib.xpt; Software: SAS
L505: Abbreviations: EOS, end of study; EOT, end of treatment; LVEF, left ventricular ejection fraction; SAS, statistical analysis software
L506: LVEF values &lt;50% as assessed by the core laboratory were observed for five subjects (4%) in the aficamten group and one subject (1%) in the placebo group. The median LVEF among the five subjects in the aficamten group was 47% (range from 34% to 49%). Of the five subjects in the aficamten group, none had associated signs or symptoms of heart failure, and none had LVEF &lt;30%. There was only one subject who had concurrent LVEF &lt;50% as assessed by both core-laboratory and site-assessed LVEF; the site assessment led to dose reduction. For the remaining 4 aficamten subjects, no dose adjustments were made because the site-assessed LVEF remained &gt;50%. Core laboratory-assessed LVEF values returned to &gt;50% during treatment for four subjects and by the end of the washout period for all five subjects.
L507: Similarly, site-assessed LVEF &lt;50% was observed for seven subjects (5%) in the aficamten group and one subject (1%) in the placebo group. All 7 aficamten-treated subjects had a subsequent dose reduction (per protocol), and all subjects had an LVEF ≥50% at the next visit. There was no treatment interruption due to LVEF &lt;50%. There was one aficamten-treated subject with reported associated signs or symptoms of heart failure. This 41-year-old White male subject reported a nonserious AE of cardiac failure with moderate intensity at Week 12 (4 weeks before a site read LVEF of 47% at Week 16). The site-assessed LVEF at Week 12 was 55%, which was decreased from 65% at Week 8 (66% at baseline). No action was taken with aficamten due to the event. The investigator assessed this AE as related to study treatment. At Week 16, aficamten was down-titrated from 20 mg to 15 mg due to site-assessed LVEF &lt;50%. About 10 days after down-titration, LVEF returned to ≥50% based on both site-read and core laboratory. The AE of cardiac failure was considered resolved.
L508: There was no specific pattern regarding the onset of LVEF ≤50%, which occurred sporadically during the entire treatment period (median onset of 97 days, range from 15 days to 169 days).
L509: 7.6.1.8. Laboratory Findings
L510: Analyses of laboratory data from the CY 6031 trial did not raise major safety concerns. Mean changes from baseline to the end of treatment were similar between the groups for most clinical chemistry and hematology parameters collected in the study. The outlier analyses did not reveal signals of concern. There was one subject in the aficamten group who had elevated creatine phosphokinase (CPK) (&gt;10× upper limit of normal [ULN]). The considerably high level of CPK was observed during the safety follow-up period about 20 days after the last aficamten treatment. The elevated CPK was transient and returned to normal after 2 weeks.

L534: 7.6.1.9. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury
L535: Review of liver enzyme tests demonstrated that no subject met the criteria for drug-induced liver injury assessed by concurrent alanine aminotransferase (ALT) or aspartate aminotransferase (AST) &gt;3\times ULN and total bilirubin &gt;2\times ULN (i.e., possible Hy's Law cases) in either treatment group in CY 6031 (Figure 9). Four aficamten-treated subjects had ALT and/or AST &gt;3\times ULN during the study period in Trial CY 6031 (Figure 9 and Table 36). In all four cases, the transaminase elevations were mild, transient, not associated with symptoms or increased bilirubin, and did not require treatment interruption (see narratives in Section 17.1). One subject with AST &gt;5\times ULN had an AST value of 302 U/L (7\times ULN) during the washout period (29 days after discontinuation of aficamten). His other liver tests were within the normal range, and his AST returned to within the normal range after 2 weeks with no intervention. Two aficamten-treated subjects had elevated bilirubin &gt;2\times ULN (one &gt;3\times ULN); both episodes were transient and occurred during the washout period about 4 weeks after discontinuation of aficamten. Overall, the liver laboratory data did not raise findings of concern.
L536: Figure 9 shows a screening assessment for potential cases of serious drug-induced liver injury in CY 6031. There were also no subjects who met Hy's law criteria in the dose-ranging Study CY 6021 and the open-label extension Study CY 6022.
L537: Figure 9. Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Safety Population, Trial CY 6031
L538: Figure 9: Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot. The plot shows Maximum Post-baseline Bilirubin (xULN) on the Y-axis (0 to 4) versus Maximum Post-baseline ALT or AST (xULN) on the X-axis (0 to 8). Data points are categorized by treatment: Aficamten (blue diamonds) and Placebo (open circles). The plot is divided into four quadrants by dashed lines at x=3 and y=2. The top-left quadrant is labeled 'Cholestasis', the top-right 'Potential Hy's Law', and the bottom-right 'Temple's Corollary'. Most data points are clustered in the bottom-left quadrant, indicating no significant liver injury. A few points in the top-left quadrant indicate cholestasis, but none meet the criteria for Hy's Law or Temple's Corollary.
L539: Source: adlb.xpt; Software: R
L540: Each data point represents a subject plotted by their maximum ALT or AST versus their maximum total bilirubin values in the postbaseline period.
L541: A potential Hy's Law case was defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2\times ULN after a postbaseline ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3\times ULN. Those subjects who met total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2\times ULN criteria within 30 days of the ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3\times ULN criteria are circled in red.
L542: The within 30 days analysis window rule does not apply to cholestasis and temple's corollary cases.
L543: All subjects with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST, bilirubin and ULN are plotted.

L556: 7.6.1.10. Vital Signs
L557: Analyses of vital signs data from CY 6031 found a greater mean change from baseline in systolic/diastolic blood pressure (SBP/DBP) in the aficamten group compared to placebo (Figure 10 and Figure 11). The mean increases were sustained throughout the treatment period with ~2 to 4 mm Hg increase from baseline in the aficamten group compared to a 1 to 3 mm Hg decrease from baseline in the placebo group.
L558: Subjects with potentially clinically significant SBP and DBP are summarized in Table 37 and Table 38, respectively. Elevated postbaseline SBP (≥160 mm Hg) was observed in approximately 16% of subjects in the aficamten group and 8% of subjects in the placebo group. Similarly, incidence of elevated postbaseline DBP (≥90 mm Hg) was higher in the aficamten group compared to placebo (30% versus 20%). The shift tables by baseline SBP and DBP are listed in Table 39 and Table 40 and do not raise additional concerns. All 11 subjects in the aficamten group who reported hypertension AEs (Table 33) had at least one post-baseline measurement of SBP ≥140 mm Hg.

L588: 7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
L589: 7.7.1. Risk of Heart Failure Due to Systolic Dysfunction
L590: Issue
L591: The Applicant proposed to manage the iatrogenic risk of HF due to systolic dysfunction via labeling and voluntary education materials without a REMS program at the time of the NDA submission. Whether the proposed strategy is sufficient to ensure the safe use of aficamten was a review issue.
L592: Background
L593: Systolic dysfunction is the identified risk for cardiac myosin inhibitors based on the mechanism of action. The first-in-class cardiac myosin inhibitor was approved in 2022 with a REMS to mitigate the risk of HF due to systolic dysfunction. The objectives of the REMS were to ensure periodic echocardiograms for dosing and monitoring and to screen for drug interactions that would increase exposure, thus increasing the risk of systolic dysfunction. The Applicant stated that aficamten has a PK and PD profile that minimizes the risk of iatrogenic systolic HF. The available clinical data to date also demonstrate a low risk of HF and/or systolic dysfunction. In light of these findings and the burden associated with a REMS, the Applicant proposed to mitigate the risk of HF due to systolic dysfunction without a REMS for aficamten.
L594: Assessment
L595: Clinical Pharmacologic Features of Aficamten
L596: Due to its complex pharmacologic profile, the first-in-class cardiac myosin inhibitor requires implementation of a complicated and structured dosing titration algorithm in conjunction with the REMS. Aficamten has a terminal half-life of ~80 hours, with steady-state concentrations achieved after 10-12 days of treatment. Aficamten undergoes elimination primarily through CYP2C9-mediated metabolism (50%) with additional contributions from CYP3A (26%), CYP2D6, and CYP2C19 pathways. The involvement of multiple CYP enzymes in aficamten elimination reduces the likelihood of clinically significant concentration increases resulting from potent inhibition of any single metabolic pathway. This suggests that large changes in aficamten concentrations are less likely to emerge from strong inhibition of any individual pathway.
L597: Currently, there are no strong CYP2C9 inhibitors commercially available in the United States. Aficamten exposure may be elevated with coadministration of strong inhibitors affecting multiple CYPs (such as fluvoxamine, fluconazole, and voriconazole, see Section 14). Based on these pharmacologic characteristics, aficamten demonstrates a moderate risk of increased exposure, potentially supporting implementation of a less restrictive dosing strategy and monitoring program.
L598: Risk of Clinical Heart Failure/Systolic Dysfunction Features of Aficamten
L599: There were no aficamten-treated oHCM subjects experiencing serious HF or severe systolic dysfunction (LVEF &lt;30%) in CY 6031 and in the open-label extension (OLE) CY 6022 study. Three SAEs of HF/ventricular dysfunction have been reported in aficamten-treated subjects with nHCM (see Narratives in Section 17.1). Table 41 summarizes the incidence and event rate of SAEs of HF/systolic dysfunction among subjects with oHCM and nHCM in the aficamten clinical programs at the time of the NDA submission for aficamten.
L600: Table 41. Incidence and Event Rate of Serious Heart Failure in the Aficamten Clinical Program

L604: Abbreviation: ER, event rate; N, number of subjects in the integrated summary of safety; n, number of subjects having an event; LVEF, left ventricular ejection fraction; nHCM, non-obstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy; oHCM, obstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy; SAE, serious adverse event
L605: Safety Updates
L606: The division requested that the Applicant report any case of SAE of HF and/or LVEF &lt;30% in all ongoing clinical trials throughout the NDA review period. No occurrences of LVEF &lt;30% have occurred in the aficamten clinical program through June 16, 2025. There were an additional six SAEs of cardiac failure in an ongoing phase 3 trial CY 6033 in subjects with nHCM (treatment assignments remain blinded) and two cases in subjects with oHCM (one on aficamten and the other on metoprolol) in the second phase 3 trial comparing the effect of aficamten versus an active control, metoprolol, in subjects with oHCM. Overall, no major concerns arose from these cases. All cases were resolved with or without treatment interruptions.
L607: Uncertainty
L608: While the risk of serious HF or severe systolic dysfunction appears to be low in aficamten-treated oHCM patients, an iatrogenic risk of HF with aficamten still exists based on the mechanism of action of the drug. The total exposure to date remains relatively low with approximately 300 patients with oHCM. There is a concern that the risk would be higher in the post-market setting where aficamten is used in a wider range of patients with higher comorbidities. It should be noted that CY 6031 excluded patients with any history of LV systolic dysfunction (LVEF &lt;45%) or stress cardiomyopathy at any time during their clinical course. It is not unreasonable to speculate that these excluded patients may be prescribed aficamten in the real world. Further, this class of drug is relatively new, and prescribers are still learning about
L609: this class and would benefit from mandated training. Careful monitoring in the real world is not expected to be as rigorous as in the clinical trials, and labeling alone will not ensure that prescribers appropriately titrate aficamten and obtain echocardiograms at appropriate time points.
L610: Conclusion
L611: The review team determined that a REMS is necessary to ensure the benefits outweigh the potential risk of HF due to systolic dysfunction. However, the review team concluded that the PK profile of aficamten does not appear to warrant the inclusion of a drug interaction monitoring component in a REMS. Dose modification related to certain strong inhibitors of multiple CYPs can be addressed via labeling. The REMS Oversight Committee concurred with this decision and recommended that prescriber education and documentation of echocardiogram monitoring must be included as part of a REMS. Hence, a REMS with ETASU consisting of ETASU A (prescriber certification), ETASU B (pharmacy certification), ETASU D (documentation of safe-use conditions), and ETASU E (patient monitoring) is required for aficamten. This decision on REMS was conveyed to the Applicant at the Mid-Cycle Communication meeting on March 4, 2025, and the Applicant subsequently submitted their proposed REMS on March 28, 2025, in response to the requirement (see the Division of Risk Management review), which was classified as a major amendment to the application and extended the PDUFA goal date by 3 months.
L612: 8. Therapeutic Individualization
L613: 8.1. Intrinsic Factors
L614: Hepatic Impairment
L615: The effect of moderate hepatic impairment (HI) on the pharmacokinetics of aficamten was evaluated in a dedicated Phase 1 HI study (CY 6017). Aficamten exposures following a single dose of aficamten 20 mg were similar in subjects with normal hepatic function and subjects with moderate HI (i.e., Child-Pugh Class B) (i.e., 38% increase in mean C_{max} and 5% decrease in mean AUC_{\infty} with moderate HI). Based on the outcome of this study, the Applicant concluded that no adjustment of the aficamten dose is required for subjects with mild or moderate HI (i.e., Child-Pugh A or B). Details about this study can be found in Section 14.2 of this review. No studies have been conducted in subjects with severe HI (i.e., Child-Pugh C).
L616: While the Clinical Pharmacology and Clinical review team believes that there would not be safety concerns with severe HI subjects as the aficamten dose will be titrated with safety monitoring, the statement of “the effects of severe hepatic (Child-Pugh Class C) impairment is unknown” has been included in the product label.
L617: Renal Impairment
L618: Based on the results of the mass balance study (CY 6013) that showed a minor role of renal function in aficamten elimination (i.e., renal clearance &lt;0.1% of total clearance), the Applicant believes that clinically significant increases in aficamten exposure due to renal impairment (RI)
L619: are unlikely and therefore, the effect of renal impairment on aficamten exposure was not evaluated in a dedicated clinical study.
L620: The Applicant evaluated the effect of mild or moderate RI on aficamten pharmacokinetics using PPK analyses based on Phase 2 and 3 study data (link to Section 14.5). The aficamten Phase 2 and 3 studies (CY 6021 and CY 6031) included subjects with oHCM that had mild (i.e., creatinine clearance [CrCL; Cockcroft-Gault equation] of 60 to 89 mL/min; N=54) or moderate (i.e., CrCL of 30 to 59 mL/min; N=13) RI. However, individuals with severe RI (i.e., estimated glomerular filtration rate [eGFR] &lt;30 mL/min/1.73 m2) were excluded from the Phase 2 and 3 studies. While evaluating the effect of renal impairment on aficamten pharmacokinetics, the Clinical Pharmacology review team noted a discrepancy between how the Applicant classified renal impairment in the inclusion/exclusion criteria for the clinical studies and how their PPK analysis was conducted. As a result, the following IR was sent to the Applicant on April 14, 2025:
L621: We note that inclusion/exclusion criteria for renal impairment in your clinical studies (e.g., Studies CY 6021 and CY 6031) were based on estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) using the modification of diet in renal disease (MDRD) equation while you conducted population PK analysis using renal impairment classification based on creatinine clearance (CLCr) derived from the Cockcroft-Gault (C-G) equation and used that for labeling. Clarify whether using different equations to estimate renal function would result in any difference in: (1) classification of renal function in the studied population; and (2) the effect of renal impairment on aficamten pharmacokinetics based on your population PK analysis.
L622: Subsequently, the Applicant submitted their response as follows on April 18, 2025 (SDN: 030): “Cytokinetics has re-assessed the impact of renal function (eGFR estimated using MDRD equation) on aficamten pharmacokinetics. When compared to the C-G-based assessment, estimation of renal function using MDRD resulted in higher number of subjects being classified as having mild or moderate renal impairment (i.e., N=54 with mild RI and N=13 with moderate RI based on C-G equation). As expected for a drug that is eliminated primarily by metabolism, aficamten pharmacokinetics was comparable among subjects with normal renal function and those with mild or moderate renal impairment (Table 42). These results agree with those using the C-G classification; thereby, supporting the use of aficamten in subjects with oHCM with mild to moderate renal impairment.” The Applicant’s response was found to be reasonable from a Clinical Pharmacology perspective.

L631: The Pharmacometrics review team assessed the Applicant's PPK analyses for the effect of mild or moderate RI on aficamten pharmacokinetics. The PPK dataset is comprised of 54 subjects with mild RI and 13 subjects with moderate RI. Based on the PPK analysis, creatinine clearance was not identified as a significant covariate affecting aficamten pharmacokinetics. Similar weight normalized clearance was observed in subjects with normal renal function and subjects with mild and moderate RI (Figure 12). The steady-state PK comparison across renal function suggested numerical increase in aficamten exposure with increasing degree of RI, which is likely due to the decreased weight and more female subjects with increasing RI in the dataset. Overall, the Applicant's proposed dosing recommendations for subjects with mild and moderate RI is reasonable.

L636: While the Clinical Pharmacology and Clinical review teams believe that there would not be safety concerns with RI subjects as aficamten dose will be titrated with safety monitoring, the statement of “the effects of severe renal (eGFR &lt;30 mL/min based on MDRD equation) impairment is unknown” has been included in the product label.
L637: Age
L638: Based on PPK-estimated aficamten exposures at steady-state following once daily (QD) dosing of 15-mg aficamten (i.e., median last stable dose in Trial CY 6031) in subjects with oHCM stratified by age group (i.e., &lt;65 years, 65 to 75 years, and &gt;75 years), a general numerical increase in aficamten exposure was observed with increasing age (i.e., age: 18 to 83 years). The Applicant believes that this result is likely due to the decreased body weight and increased proportion of female subjects with increasing age group in the dataset. The Applicant concluded that no dose adjustment of aficamten is warranted based on age. The Pharmacometrics review team found this to be reasonable.
L639: Body Weight
L640: Body weight was identified as a significant covariate on aficamten pharmacokinetics in the Applicant's PPK analysis. Population PK-estimated steady-state exposures (i.e., AUC_{tau}, C_{max}, and C_{tau}) for aficamten in subjects with oHCM were compared across body weight quartiles. Body weight was predicted to have a modest effect on aficamten pharmacokinetics. Aficamten plasma AUC_{tau}, C_{max}, and C_{tau} in a subject with oHCM at the fourth (i.e., highest) weight quartile
L641: (i.e., 95 to 156 kg) were 34%, 35%, and 33% lower, respectively, compared to a subject with oHCM at the first (i.e., lowest) weight quartile (i.e., 47 to 70 kg). The Applicant believes that these differences are expected from simple allometry and the span of median weight between the fourth and first weight quartiles. The Applicant concludes that no dose adjustment of aficamten is warranted based on body weight. The Applicant believes that the proposed individualized aficamten dose titration strategy based on achievement of target LVOT-G and maintenance of normal LVEF is expected to mitigate any effect of body weight on aficamten pharmacokinetics. The Pharmacometrics review team found this to be reasonable.
L642: Sex
L643: Sex was identified as a significant covariate on aficamten pharmacokinetics in the Applicant's PPK analysis. Population PK-estimated steady-state exposures (i.e., AUC_{tau}, C_{max}, and C_{tau}) for aficamten in subjects with oHCM were compared by male versus female (i.e., 105 males and 78 females). Sex was predicted to have a modest effect on aficamten pharmacokinetics. Aficamten plasma AUC_{tau}, C_{max}, and C_{tau} in female subjects with oHCM were 31%, 34%, and 30% higher, respectively, relative to male subjects with oHCM. The Applicant concludes that no dose adjustment of aficamten is warranted based on sex considering that the general practice of echocardiography-based aficamten dose titration is expected to mitigate any effect of sex on aficamten pharmacokinetics. The Pharmacometrics review team found this to be reasonable.
L644: Ethnicity
L645: The impact of ethnicity on the pharmacokinetics of aficamten was evaluated via the Applicant's PPK analyses using the Phase 2 (Study CY 6021) and Phase 3 (Trial CY 6031) data. No impact of ethnicity on aficamten pharmacokinetics was identified in the PPK analysis. Therefore, the Applicant concludes that no dose adjustment of aficamten is warranted based on ethnicity. The Pharmacometrics review team found this to be reasonable.
L646: Overall Recommendation
L647: The Clinical Pharmacology review team agrees that no dose adjustment for aficamten is warranted for intrinsic factors such as HI, RI, age, body weight, sex, and ethnicity that are described above.
L648: 8.2. Extrinsic Factors
L649: Food
L650: The effect of food on aficamten pharmacokinetics was assessed in a dedicated food effect study (CY 6012) conducted with the to-be-marketed (TBM) tablet formulation to support that aficamten may be administered without regard to food. No clinically significant differences in aficamten exposure (i.e., AUC and C_{max}) were observed following its administration with a high-fat, high-calorie meal. Details of the study can be found in Section 14.2. Aficamten was administered without regard to food in the pivotal Phase 3 trial, CY 6031.
L651: Drug-Drug Interactions
L652: Effect of Other Drugs on Aficamten Pharmacokinetics
L653: The DDI potential of aficamten was evaluated in vitro and in clinical studies.
L654: In Vitro DDI Data
L655: Based on the in vitro studies, aficamten is metabolized primarily through CYP2C9 with contributions by CYP3A4, CYP2C19, and CYP2D6. Aficamten is not a substrate of drug transporters.
L656: Clinical DDI Data
L657: Based on the in vitro findings, several clinical DDI studies were conducted including those evaluating the effects of strong CYP3A inhibition (i.e., itraconazole), CYP3A and CYP2C9 induction (i.e., carbamazepine) (Study CY 6014); strong inhibition of CYP2C19 and moderate inhibition of CYP2C9 and CYP3A (i.e., fluconazole), strong inhibition of CYP2D6 (i.e., paroxetine), and strong inhibition of CYP2C19 and CYP2D6 (i.e., fluoxetine) (Study CY 601-10) on the pharmacokinetics of aficamten (Table 43). Results from in vitro and clinical DDI assessments indicate that aficamten is primarily eliminated via CYP2C9-mediated metabolism (50%) with contributions from CYP3A (26%), CYP2C19, and CYP2D6.

L663: Physiologically Based PK Modeling
L664: To further understand the effects of CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A inhibition and CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP3A induction on aficamten pharmacokinetics, a physiologically
L665: based PK (PBPK) model was developed by the Applicant to supplement the clinical DDI studies and provide a comprehensive evaluation of DDI effects on aficamten's pharmacokinetics (see Section 14.5.3).
L666: In their PBPK report, CYT/11/B, the Applicant stated that their primary model f_m values for CYP2C9 and CYP3A4 can be considered verified, but there is still some uncertainty for the f_m values for CYP2C19 and CYP2D6. The over-prediction of aficamten pharmacokinetics in CYP2D6 poor metabolizers (PMs) and the underprediction of the fluconazole DDI may both indicate a potential greater role of CYP2C19 (i.e., estimated maximum f_m=24%) and lesser role for CYP2D6 (i.e., f_m=0%). A sensitivity analysis performed using an exploratory model with CYP2D6 mediated metabolism removed and replaced by CYP2C19 mediated metabolism was also presented in their PBPK report (CYT/11/B). As expected, this model more accurately describes the pharmacokinetics in CYP2D6 PMs (i.e., no change from non-PMs) and the fluconazole DDI (i.e., predicted within 1.1-fold of the observed data) but no longer captures the effect of paroxetine DDI. As such, the Applicant relied on a model (i.e., primary model) that includes a significant contribution by CYP2D6 to align with in vitro results and accurately capture the results of the dedicated DDI study with paroxetine for prospective DDI prediction.
L667: While evaluating the Applicant's PBPK modeling, the Clinical Pharmacology PBPK reviewer further modified the Applicant's exploratory model by assigning fm_{CYP2D6} to 3% and fm_{CYP2C19} to 21% and making further necessary adjustments (see Section 14.5.3). As a result, the reviewer's model successfully predicted all changes in aficamten AUCs observed in clinical studies including paroxetine. The mean AUC changes predicted by the aforementioned three different PBPK models are summarized in Table 44. In addition, different phenotypes of CYP2C9 (normal metabolizers [NMs] and PMs) were considered to account for the worst-case scenario for aficamten exposure increase. For example, we compared aficamten AUC in CYP2C9 PMs in the presence of an inhibitor to aficamten AUC in CYP2C9 NMs in the absence of the inhibitor. The combined inhibition effect (i.e., aficamten AUC increase in PMs in the presence of an inhibitor) will be a product of multiplying the baseline difference between PMs and NMs with the exposure increase resulting from the coadministration with an inhibitor. For CYP2C9, the effect of sulfaphenazole (i.e., strong CYP2C9 inhibitor) on aficamten AUC in CYP2C9 NMs was considered as the baseline difference in AUC between CYP2C9 NMs and PMs (i.e., 2.13-fold). For example, aficamten AUC in CYP2C9 PMs following coadministration with fluvoxamine 150-mg BID for 15 days is predicted to be approximately 4.9-fold higher compared to CYP2C9 NMs not taking fluvoxamine when using the reviewer's model in Table 44 (i.e., 2.13 \text{ [NM versus PM baseline difference]} \times 2.28 \text{ [aficamten AUC increase with fluvoxamine]}).

L680: Source: FDA reviewer's analysis
L681: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time-curve; BID, twice daily; CYP, cytochrome P450; LVEF, left ventricular ejection fraction; LVOT-G, left ventricular outflow tract gradient; PBPK, physiologically based pharmacokinetic; QD, once daily
L682: The Applicant proposed to contraindicate aficamten administration with more than 3 days of fluconazole administration, regardless of CYP2C9 phenotype. This is based on the observation that fluconazole 400-mg QD increased aficamten exposure by 3.8-fold in CYP2C9 and CYP2C19 extensive, rapid, or ultra-rapid metabolizers (Study CY 601-10). As shown in Table 45 above, the combined effects of multiple CYP enzyme inhibition and CYP2C9 PM status for voriconazole and fluconazole are predicted to result in a 7.5-fold and 7.6-fold higher AUC, respectively. Therefore, when initiating voriconazole or fluconazole in subjects already taking aficamten, the aficamten dose should be reduced to 5 mg (i.e., 3 to 4-fold dose decrease from 15 mg or 20-mg dose) considering approximately 3.5-fold AUC increase expected by initiating these inhibitors. Aficamten is expected to have more than a 3-fold therapeutic window based on the Phase 3 trial (CY 6031) outcome. The proposed 3 to 4-fold aficamten dose reduction is expected to appropriately mitigate any resulting additional aficamten exposure increases due to the initiation of these inhibitors. However, those on 5 mg or 10-mg aficamten should avoid concomitant use of voriconazole or fluconazole as reduction of the aficamten dose may not appropriately mitigate the risk resulting from aficamten exposure increase.
L683: At the same time, the Applicant proposes to allow \leq 3 days of concomitant fluconazole to enable the common usage of fluconazole in the outpatient setting for the treatment of uncomplicated vulvovaginal candidiasis. In this setting, fluconazole is administered as a single 150-mg dose (Day 1). If the infection does not resolve within 48 hours, a second 150-mg dose (Day 3) can be given. The effect of 2 \times 150-mg fluconazole dose, separated by 48 hours, on stable aficamten therapy (i.e., steady state 15-mg QD) was evaluated via PBPK modeling; and it was predicted that this regimen will only elicit a minimal effect on aficamten exposure (i.e., maximum increase of 10% AUC over the dosing interval). The Clinical Pharmacology review team found the Applicant's proposal to be reasonable.
L684: The aficamten dose should be reduced 2 to 3-fold when initiating fluvoxamine or sulfaphenazole. In the same manner as when initiating inhibitors such as voriconazole or fluconazole, a 2 to 3-fold aficamten dose reduction is expected to appropriately mitigate any resulting additional aficamten exposure increase due to the initiation of these inhibitors in subjects already taking aficamten considering that less than a 2.3-fold mean AUC increase is expected by initiating these inhibitors.
L685: When initiating aficamten in subjects already taking the above-mentioned inhibitors, aficamten should be initiated at 5 mg with LVEF and LVOT-G monitoring and the dose should be increased further if needed based on treatment response. A starting dose of 5 mg is expected to be safe for aficamten despite an approximately 2-fold decrease expected in aficamten clearance due to inhibitor presence as there would not be any additional exposure increase due to the initiation of aficamten therapy as subjects are already on stable therapy with the above-mentioned inhibitors. Any subsequent aficamten dose up-titration while on inhibitor therapy can be safely guided by echocardiographic assessment to maintain LVEF &gt; 50\%.
L686: To assess the potential effect of CYP inducers on aficamten pharmacokinetics, the Applicant conducted a PBPK analysis showing that 600-mg QD dosing of rifampin (i.e., strong CYP3A inducer, strong CYP2C19 inducer, and moderate CYP2C9 inducer) was predicted to reduce the mean aficamten AUC by 79%. Given the extent of predicted drug interaction with rifampin, the Applicant is proposing to contraindicate the concomitant use of aficamten with rifampin as the reduced aficamten exposure may substantially reduce efficacy.
L687: NDA 219083MYQORZO (aficamten)
L688: A clinical DDI study (CY 6014) was conducted to evaluate the effect of steady state carbamazepine (i.e., moderate to strong CYP3A inducer and weak CYP2C9 inducer) exposure on aficamten single dose (i.e., 20 mg) pharmacokinetics. This study showed a 51% decrease in aficamten mean AUC. The Applicant is proposing to reduce the dose of aficamten (i.e., 20 mg → 10 mg, 15 mg → 5 mg, 10 mg → 5 mg, or maintain the dose of 5 mg if currently on 5 mg and monitor LVEF and LVOT-G) when discontinuing a moderate to strong inducer of CYP2C9 or CYP3A.
L689: The Applicant's proposal for both inducer drugs mentioned above is reasonable from a Clinical Pharmacology perspective.
L690: After careful consideration, the Clinical Pharmacology review team has concluded that the listing of CYP2C9 inducers in Section 7 (i.e., Drug Interactions) of the product label is not necessary as there are no strong CYP2C9 inducers currently on the United States market (i.e., hypothetical) and other known moderate CYP2C9 inducers would have overlap with moderate to strong CYP3A inducers.
L691: It should be noted that no significant exposure-safety relationship was identified between aficamten exposures and assessed safety endpoints such as major adverse cardiovascular events, cardiovascular adverse events, or hypertension at the evaluated dose range of 5 to 20-mg QD in the Phase 3 trial (CY 6031) and the aficamten therapeutic window is expected to be &gt;3-fold.
L692: Effect of Aficamten on Other Drugs' Pharmacokinetics
L693: The Applicant concludes that in vitro studies suggest aficamten does not substantially inhibit any of the CYP isoforms tested (i.e., CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4; IC50 values &gt;100μM), and it is predicted not to be an in vivo inducer in humans. The in vitro assessment of the inhibition potential for P-glycoprotein (P-gp), breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP), organic cation transporter 2 (OCT2), organic cation transporter 3 (OCT3), organic anion transporter 1 (OAT1), organic anion transporter 3 (OAT3), organic anion transporting polypeptide 1B1 (OATP1B1), organic anion transporting polypeptide 1B3 (OATP1B3), multidrug and toxin extrusion protein 1 (MATE1), and multidrug and toxin extrusion protein 2-K (MATE2-K) suggested that aficamten may inhibit P-gp in vivo, leading to evaluation in a dedicated clinical DDI study (Study CY 6014). Concomitant administration of aficamten increased total dabigatran (i.e., a sensitive P-gp probe) AUC∞ and Cmax by 26% and 27%, respectively (Section 14.2). Based on these results, the review team agrees with the Applicant's conclusion that aficamten may be coadministered with P-gp substrates.
L694: 8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
L695: Aficamten was granted orphan designation for the treatment of HCM on January 5, 2021. Based on the orphan designation for aficamten and in accordance with Section 505B(k)(1) of the Food, Drug, and Cosmetic (FD&amp;C) Act, a formal pediatric study plan for the population of birth to less than 18 years of age is not required. This exemption from the Pediatric Research Equity Act requirements was confirmed at the pre-NDA meeting held on February 26, 2024.
L696: 8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of Reproductive Potential
L697: Refer to the Division of Pediatrics and Maternal Health review dated June 4, 2025, and the summary of key nonclinical data (Table 46) supporting the labeling sections on fertility, pregnancy and lactation.
L698: A rat embryofetal development (EFD) study demonstrated an increase in external malformations (kinked tail) at aficamten exposures higher than clinical exposures at the MRHD. This finding is of uncertain clinical relevance.
L699: In a rabbit EFD study, a numerical imbalance in cardiac findings was observed between the mid-dose and vehicle control groups (two litters versus one, respectively). The findings consisted of increased cardiac malformations (persistent truncus arteriosus, misshapen heart, and absent ventricular septum) that were slightly above the historical control incidence. The findings were not observed with a clear dose-response (even when accounting for the loss of some litters at the highest dose). Furthermore, all three malformations also occurred in control litters, which indicates the findings are not rare (i.e., they occurred at 5 to 6% in the historical control database, a level exceeding a threshold of &gt;1 to 2%). Furthermore, imbalances in cardiac malformations were not observed in fetuses in the rat EFD study. Taken together, while a direct relationship between aficamten and cardiac malformations cannot be entirely excluded (due primarily to aficamten’s mechanism of action), the available data are inadequate to establish a causal relationship. Given the signal cannot be distinguished from a chance finding, we recommend that residual concern be mitigated through disclosure of the findings in product labeling.
L700: Animal fertility studies do not demonstrate any adverse effect at clinical exposures, therefore, aficamten is not predicted to impair human fertility.
L701: Given the limited human data on aficamten use in pregnancy and the absence of data regarding aficamten presence in human breast milk, the Division of Pediatrics and Maternal Health recommends issuing two post marketing requirements to conduct studies that collect pregnancy outcome data in humans and determine aficamten concentrations in human breast milk (see Section 24).
L702: Table 46. Nonclinical Data Supporting Labeling on Fertility, Pregnancy, and Lactation
L703: Labeling SectionNonclinical Data



Pregnancy
Administration of aficamten during organogenesis in rats was associated with increased early and late fetal resorptions, decreased mean fetal body weight, and an increased incidence of fetuses with kinked tails at 9 mg/kg/day, approximately 5-times the clinical exposure at MRHD based on free AUC. Maternal toxicity characterized by decreased body weight gain during gestation, decreased food consumption, and increased heart weight, was observed at the same dose.
Administration of aficamten during organogenesis in rabbits was associated with a numerical increase in postimplantation loss (late resorptions) and fetuses with various cardiac malformations at the mid-dose (10 mg/kg) only. The high dose (20/15 mg/kg/day) caused maternal toxicity and mortalities at a clinically relevant exposure level.
Administration of aficamten to pregnant rats during embryofetal organogenesis through offspring weaning in a pre- and postnatal development study caused reduced offspring viability and reduced pup birth weight at the highest dose (6 mg/kg/day) in the presence of maternal toxicities (decreased maternal body weight gain and increased maternal heart weight), at about 4-times the clinical exposure at the MRHD. An increased number of offspring with bent tails was also observed at the same dose. No adverse maternal toxicity or developmental effects occurred at the mid-dose at clinical exposure level. Aficamten did not affect neurobehavioral parameters, sexual maturation, or reproductive function of the offspring at any dose.


8.2 Lactation
In a rat PPND study, aficamten was detected in nursing offspring when administered to lactating mothers from gestation through postnatal day 4 at \geq 0.5 mg/kg/day.


13.1 Impairment of Fertility
Aficamten exposure did not impact fertility, mating frequency or index, estrus, sperm quality, or histopathology of reproductive organs in both male and female in rats, at up to 6 mg/kg/day in females and 3 mg/kg/day in males.

L717: III. Additional Analyses and Information
L718: 12. Summary of Regulatory History
L719: The IND opening study, CY 6011, entitled “A Phase 1, Double-blind, Randomized, Placebo-controlled, Multi-part, Single and Multiple-Ascending Dose Study of CK-3773274 in Healthy Adult Subjects” was submitted on October 3, 2018, and a study may proceed letter was issued on November 2, 2018.
L720: Aficamten received Orphan Drug Designation on January 5, 2021, for the treatment of symptomatic hypertrophic cardiomyopathy. Aficamten also was granted breakthrough therapy designation on December 6, 2021.
L721: Trial CY 6031 (SEQUOIA-HCM), entitled "A Phase 3, Multi-Center, Randomized, Double-blind, Placebo-Controlled Trial to Evaluate the Efficacy and Safety of CK-3773274 in Adults with Symptomatic oHCM" was conducted globally, including in the United States. The enrollment of Trial CY 6031 was completed on May 26, 2023. While there were no interim data locks, the protocol specified that the Applicant would monitor the aggregate pooled missing data and overall pooled standard deviation for the change in baseline in pVO2 at Week 24 (the primary endpoint). The Applicant did not lock the database to perform this monitoring, nor did they adjust the sample size. The results of CY 6031 (SEQUOIA-HCM) and the additional safety data from the open-label extension Study CY 6022 (FOREST-HCM), to support the NDA submission for aficamten in patients with symptomatic oHCM were discussed with the division at the topline meeting on February 8, 2024 (minutes dated February 29, 2024), and the pre-NDA meeting held on February 26, 2024 (minutes dated March 26, 2024). The review team concluded the information outlined in the pre-NDA meeting package appeared sufficient to conduct a filing review of the NDA and accepted a rolling review with the clinical and nonclinical modules ahead of the full NDA submission.
L722: On September 26, 2024, Cytokinetics Inc. submitted the final portion of the Rolling submission of their NDA for aficamten for the following proposed indication: “treatment of adults with symptomatic obstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (oHCM)”.
L723: Please see the key interactions with the Agency over the course of development, provided in Table 47.

L728: 13. Pharmacology Toxicology
L729: 13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With the Investigational New Drug Application
L730: Aficamten is a small molecule inhibitor of cardiac myosin (Myh7) designed to decrease cardiomyocyte contractility and relax the cardiac muscle for the treatment of oHCM. Results from in vitro and in vivo primary pharmacology studies support the proposed mechanism of action, the results of which are summarized in Table 48. The selectivity and dose-response profiles of aficamten and its metabolites were investigated using purified myofibrils, cultured cardiac cells, in engineered cardiac tissue, and in both healthy animals and animal models of oHCM.
L731: Table 48. Primary Pharmacology Studies
L732: StudyFindings



In vitro PD studies of aficamten and its major metabolitesAficamten inhibited ATPase activity in bovine cardiac (IC_{50}=1.26\mu\text{M}) and slow muscle myofibrils (IC_{50}=1.23\mu\text{M}), but its major metabolites did not1 (Nonclin-0019, Nonclin-0460). Aficamten was 5-fold less active against rabbit fast muscle myofibrils (IC_{50}=6.5\mu\text{M}) and inactive1 against chicken smooth muscle (Nonclin-0019). Rat ventricular cardiomyocyte contraction was inhibited when cultured with aficamten (Nonclin-0019). Aficamten reduced the contractility of engineered heart tissues composed of induced human pluripotent stem cell-derived cardiomyocytes from wild-type (IC_{50}=1\mu\text{M}) and MYH7 R403Q heterozygous donors (IC_{50}=1.65\mu\text{M}) (Nonclin-0456).
L733: StudyFindings



In vivo PD studies of aficamten in healthy animals
Rat:

A single oral dose to healthy rats (0.5, 1, 2, 4 mg/kg), resulted in a dose-dependent reduction in LVFS (ranging from 20% to 70% reduction from baseline, at the mean plasma concentration of approximately 1.1 to 13\muM) [IC_{50}=8\muM] which reversed 24 hours after dosing (Nonclin-0037).
Daily oral doses to healthy rats for 10 days (1, 3, 6 mg/kg/day) resulted in dose-dependent reduction in LVFS (ranging from 13 to 25% reduction in the low dose group to 75 to 79% reduction in the high dose group, relative to the baseline, at the mean plasma C_{max} of approximately 2.5\muM to 16.1\muM). There was no significant difference in LVFS change between Day 4 and Day 10, and the LVFS of all dose groups returned to baseline 24 hours after the last dose (Nonclin-0038).

Dog:

A single oral dose of aficamten (0.75, 2, 3 mg/kg) reduced LVFS dose-dependently, from a 15% reduction relative to baseline at 0.75 mg/kg (0.3\muM) to a 50% reduction at 3 mg/kg (IC_{50}=1.27\muM), which partially recovered (to ~85% of pre-dosing value) by 48 hours (Nonclin-0007).



Study to assess the effect of IV administration of dobutamine on cardiac contractility in healthy rats administered an oral single dose of aficamtenIntravenous infusion of dobutamine dose-dependently (3.3-10 \mug/kg/min) reversed the LVFS reduction induced by a single 2 mg/kg dose of aficamten after 5 minutes in rats (Nonclin-0049).

In vivo PD studies of aficamten in HCM models.
Mouse model:

Aficamten dose-dependently reduced LVFS in wild type and a MYH7 R403Q transgenic mouse model of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, after a single dose (0.25, 0.5, 1, 1.25 and 1.5 mg/kg). The interpolated plasma concentration at 10% reduction of LVFS relative to baseline (IC_{10}) for WT and R403Q mice was 0.12\muM and 0.11\muM, respectively. The 50% reduction in LVFS (IC_{50}) for WT and R403Q mice was 0.90\muM and 0.78\muM, respectively. (Nonclin-0057).

Cat model:

Aficamten dose-dependently reduced LVFS and improved left ventricular outflow tract pressure gradient by up to &gt;90% at a dose of 2 mg/kg in a cat genetic model of oHCM (Nonclin-0141).
L734: Source: Pharm/Tox reviewer's table
L735: 1IC_{50} values were &gt;39\muM, the highest dose used in each study.

L742: 13.1.1. Safety Pharmacology
L743: A complete battery of safety pharmacology studies was conducted to characterize any effects of aficamten and its metabolites on the human ether-à-go-go related gene (hERG) channel, cardiovascular, respiratory, and central nervous system. Effects observed on electrocardiogram readings after repeated aficamten dosing are typical of cardiac compensation due to the intended pharmacologic action of aficamten. Aficamten and its metabolites did not have inhibitory effects on the hERG channel at clinically relevant concentrations. The safety pharmacology studies are summarized in Table 50 below.
L744: Table 50. Safety Pharmacology Studies
L745: StudyFindings



In vitro hERG channel inhibition assay for aficamten and its major metabolitesThe in vitro IC50 for aficamten against hERG was not determined due to insolubility of the drug above 10µM (3,400 ng/mL). At 3µM and 10µM aficamten, hERG was inhibited by 3% and 3.7% respectively (Nonclin-0003). IC50 values for the major metabolites of aficamten were estimated to be &gt;30M (10,600 ng/mL) for M1a and 51µM (18,068 ng/mL) for M1b. At 10µM, M1b inhibited hERG by 18% (Nonclin-0292, Nonclin-0298).

Repeat-dose telemetry study to assess the effect of aficamten on cardiovascular functionDaily administration of aficamten at doses up to 2 mg/kg for 1-week in telemetered dogs resulted in transient cardiovascular effects (Nonclin-0013). These included:

Increased heart rate (up to 44% increase)
Decreased systolic blood pressure (up to 30% decrease)
Decreased dP/dt (up to 25% decrease)
Increased left ventricular end-diastolic pressure (up to 37% increase)

These effects were expected and reflect the pharmacological action of aficamten or cardiac compensation in response to the drug's pharmacologic effects. All observed changes resolved within 6 hours after dosing.

Single-dose study to assess the effect of aficamten on respiratory systemAficamten caused an increase in respiratory rate accompanied by a slight decrease in tidal volume lasting at least 4 hours in rats at 9 mg/kg, which reversed by 24 hours after dosing (Nonclin-0012).
L746: StudyFindingsSingle-dose study to assess the effect of aficamten on central nervous system functionDecreased body temperature and decreased alertness were observed, which reversed by 24 hours after dosing at 9 mg/kg in male rats (Nonclin-0011).

L749: 13.1.2. Pharmacokinetics
L750: The pharmacokinetics and absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion (ADME) of aficamten were characterized in in vitro assays and in vivo studies using pharmacologically relevant species that included the dog, rat, rabbit, mouse, and monkey. Toxicokinetics were assessed in toxicology studies in rats, dogs, rabbits, and mice. Overall, these data confirm the clinical relevance of these nonclinical models for human risk assessment for aficamten.
L751: 13.1.2.1. Absorption and Distribution
L752: Absorption of aficamten after oral dosing was comparable between rats, dogs, and primates. Absolute oral bioavailability was ~60% for rats, ~40% for dogs and non-human primates, and was not determined for humans. Time to maximum concentration (Tmax) in humans after dosing with the clinical formulation was 1.5 to 2 hours, which was slightly longer than Tmax in rats and dogs dosed by oral gavage (~0.5 to 1 hour). Steady-state volume of distribution between species varied widely, from 0.5 L/kg in rats to 11 L/kg in dogs and 8 L/kg in non-human primates. No significant differences in aficamten exposure between males and females for any species were observed.
L753: Plasma protein binding for aficamten and its major metabolites (M1a and M1b) were determined for several species in vitro. Aficamten plasma protein binding was 98%, 90%, and 75% in rats, rabbits, and dogs, respectively. M1a and M1b protein binding was ~86% and ~89% in rats, and ~45% and 43% in dogs, respectively. Human plasma protein binding in vitro for aficamten, M1a, and M1b were ~90%, ~83%, and ~93% respectively. To account for the differences in plasma protein binding between species, exposure margins for aficamten were calculated based on unbound (free) Cmax or AUC in addition to total Cmax and total AUC.
L754: After a single oral dose in fasted rats, aficamten was detected in the heart, plasma, liver, and gastrocnemius muscle, with very low levels detected in the cerebrospinal fluid and brain. The tissue with highest exposure to aficamten was the heart, the site of aficamten's pharmacological action, with a tissue-to-plasma ratio of ~1.4, followed by the gastrocnemius with a ratio of 0.7. Notably, the heart was the primary organ of toxicity in animal toxicology studies.
L755: Placental and milk transfer of aficamten was not directly assessed in embryofetal development study or pre- and postnatal development studies.
L756: 13.1.2.2. Metabolism and Excretion
L757: After oral dosing, aficamten is metabolized more quickly in animals as compared to humans. Aficamten's half-life was 25 hours in rats, 79 hours in dogs, and 144 hours in human hepatocytes
L758: in vitro. Half-life measurements across species in vivo are ~80 hours, ~28 hours, and ~3 hours, in humans, dogs, and rats, respectively. Drug accumulation was evident in repeat-dose studies in dogs by exposure ratios of ~2 when comparing AUC and ~1.5 when comparing C_{max} on the final day of dosing to the first day. Daily dosing in the clinic resulted in drug accumulation in humans as well, with an exposure ratio of ~5 based on AUC and ~2 based on C_{max}, consistent with longer half-life in humans.
L759: In all species, the major circulating metabolites of aficamten were M1a and M1b (also known as CK-3834282 and CK-3834283, respectively), diastereomers of (\omega-1)-hydroxylation of the ethyl group on the ethyl-1,2,4-oxadiazol-3-yl moiety of aficamten, formed by CYP enzymes. In humans, metabolite to parent ratios for M1a and M1b were 74% and 129%, respectively. These two metabolites are also major metabolites of aficamten identified in the rat in vivo, but at lower levels. In rats, the metabolite to parent ratios for M1a and M1b were ~9% and 29%, respectively. No unique human metabolites were identified in vitro using liver microsomes or in vivo, making the rat and dog metabolically relevant species for toxicology studies.
L760: M1a and M1b were inactive in all assays tested. In vitro PD assays and 14-day repeat dose toxicology studies in rats with the major metabolites of aficamten demonstrated their relative pharmacologic inactivity. In an in vitro study, M1b inhibited bovine cardiac myofibril ATPase activity by 15% at 18\muM, but no IC_{50} could be determined for M1a or M1b up to maximum concentrations of 39\muM, demonstrating lower potency by at least 30-fold, compared with aficamten (IC_{50}=1.26\muM), which is anticipated to be pharmacologically inactive at therapeutic exposures of aficamten. M1a or M1b singly administered to rats for 14 days at 50 mg/kg/day had no toxicologically significant effects.
L761: M1a and M1b were measured in aficamten repeat dose studies in rats and dogs. Exposures to each metabolite relative to clinical levels are summarized in Table 51.
